<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> For every fixed <i>C</i> &gt; 1, approximating Min-UnCut within factor <i>C</i> is NP-hard, even on simple undirected unweighted graphs.

- **C01** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): For every fixed integer $K\ge2$, there is a deterministic polynomial-time many-one reduction mapping a $3$SAT formula $\varphi$ to a pair $(G,k)$, where $G$ is an explicit finite simple undirected unweighted graph and $k\ge1$ is a binary-encoded integer, such that $$\begin{aligned} \varphi\text{ satisfiable}&\quad\Longrightarrow\quad \operatorname{OPT}_{\rm uncut}(G)\le k,\\ \varphi\text{ unsatisfiable}&\quad\Longrightarrow\quad \operatorname{OPT}_{\rm uncut}(G)>Kk. \end{aligned}$$ For each fixed $K$, the graph size, full construction work, and bit complexity are polynomial in the bit length of $\varphi$. All constants are effective; no advice or oracle is supplied to the reduction.
  - Explanation: An effective reduction creates a strict positive-threshold multiplicative gap on simple unweighted graphs for each fixed requested factor.
- **C02** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-min-2cnf-deletion)): For every fixed real $C>1$, minimum $2$CNF clause deletion is NP-hard to approximate within factor $C$. In particular, a deterministic polynomial-time $C$-approximation would imply $\mathsf P=\mathsf{NP}$.
  - Explanation: The graph gap transfers to minimum clause deletion while preserving optimum cost.
- **C03** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-odd-labels)): The map $a\mapsto\operatorname{ev}_a$, where $\operatorname{ev}_a(b)=b(a)$, is a bijection from $A$ onto the odd frequencies of $D(A)$. If $\pi:A\to A'$ is affine and $\pi^*:D(A')\to D(A)$ is pullback, then its dual sends $\operatorname{ev}_a$ to $\operatorname{ev}_{\pi(a)}$.
  - Explanation: Evaluation identifies affine labels with odd dual frequencies and respects affine pullback.
- **C04** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-max3lin)): For every fixed rational $\zeta>0$, there is a deterministic polynomial-time reduction from $3$SAT to a nonempty explicit list of equations $x_i+x_j+x_k=b$ over $\mathbb F_2$, with uniform sampling from the list, such that satisfiable inputs admit an assignment satisfying at least $1-\zeta$ of the list and unsatisfiable inputs admit none satisfying more than $3/4$. The list length and the full bit complexity are polynomial for fixed $\zeta$.
  - Explanation: The unconditional parity-equation reduction provides a near-satisfiable completeness case and a bounded soundness case.
- **C05** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-parallel-repetition)): There is an effectively specified constant $c<1$ such that every finite game with at most eight first-player answers, at most two second-player answers, and value at most $11/12$ has $r$-fold repeated value at most $c^r$ for all integers $r\ge0$. The same $c$ works for all question distributions and all question-set sizes.
  - Explanation: Ordinary repetition decreases the value uniformly over question distributions and question-set sizes.
- **C06** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-honest)): For any choice of labels $a_\xi\in A_\xi$, the evaluation proofs $f_\xi(P)=P(a_\xi)$ satisfy $\varepsilon_j\le b_j$ for $j=1,2,3$. In fact, $$\varepsilon_1\le 2\sigma,\qquad \varepsilon_2\le2\delta+2\eta,\qquad \varepsilon_3=\|\mathcal C\|^{-1}.$$
  - Explanation: Evaluation proofs meet each comparison budget, including exact truth-table failure probability.
- **C07** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-inner)): For every rational $J\ge1$, there are effectively selectable integers $m,n\ge2$ and positive rational constants $\sigma,\delta,\eta,p,\theta<1$, with $\eta\le\sigma$, depending only on $J$, with the following property. For any finite weighted family of nonempty affine alphabets and folded bit proofs, suppose $$\varepsilon_j\le Jb_j\qquad(j=1,2,3).$$ Draw $\xi$, a standard Gaussian $c\in\mathbb R^X$, independent uniform $x\in X$ and $i\in[m]$, and all background rows independently and uniformly in $D(A_\xi)$. With probability at least $p$, the function $$b\longmapsto h_x(B[B^i_{x_{-i}}\leftarrow b],c)$$ has a Fourier coefficient of absolute value at least $\theta$ at an odd frequency of $D(A_\xi)$. Equivalently, the event is $$\label{eq:inner-atom-event} \max_{\alpha\in D(A_\xi)^*: \alpha(1)=1} \left\|\mathbb E_{b\in D(A_\xi)} h_x(B[B^i_{x_{-i}}\leftarrow b],c)(-1)^{\alpha(b)}\right\| \ge\theta.$$ For every folded proof, without any test assumption, one has pointwise $$\label{eq:gradient-bounds} \lVert h(B,c)\rVert_{2,x}\le\sigma^{-1},\qquad \|h_x(B,c)\|\le\sqrt N/\sigma.$$
  - Explanation: Averaged low comparison cost forces an odd conditional row coefficient with alphabet-independent probability and magnitude.
- **C08** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-smoothing-cutoffs)): Assume $\varepsilon_1\le Jb_1$ and $\varepsilon_2\le Jb_2$, and use <a href="#eq:smoothing-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:smoothing-parameters">[eq:smoothing-parameters]</a>–<a href="#eq:hermite-cutoff-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hermite-cutoff-choice">[eq:hermite-cutoff-choice]</a>. For every $m,n\ge2$, $$\begin{aligned} \mathbb E\lVert h\rVert_{2,x}^2&\ge\frac34,\label{eq:gradient-lower}\\ \mathbb E\lVert(I-\Pi_c)h\rVert_{2,x}^2&\le\frac1{16},\label{eq:c-tail}\\ \mathbb E\lVert(I-\Pi_B)h\rVert_{2,x}^2&\le\frac1{32},\label{eq:B-tail}\\ \mathbb E\langle L,h\rangle_x=\mathbb E\lVert L\rVert_{2,x}^2&\ge\frac12, \qquad \mathbb E\lVert L\rVert_{2,x}^2\le\sigma^{-2}. \label{eq:L-energy} \end{aligned}$$
  - Explanation: Smoothing preserves gradient energy while row and Hermite projections discard controlled energy.
- **C09** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-correlation)): Fix $J$ and the constants <a href="#eq:smoothing-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:smoothing-parameters">[eq:smoothing-parameters]</a>–<a href="#eq:hermite-cutoff-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hermite-cutoff-choice">[eq:hermite-cutoff-choice]</a>. For every $m,n\ge2$ and every finite weighted family of folded proofs satisfying $\varepsilon_j\le Jb_j$ for $j=1,2,3$, the field $L$ from <a href="#eq:low-degree-field" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:low-degree-field">[eq:low-degree-field]</a> has Fourier row degree at most $Rm$ and Gaussian degree at most $s$, satisfies <a href="#eq:L-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L-energy">[eq:L-energy]</a>, and obeys $$\label{eq:correlation-lower} \mathbb E\mathfrak d_X(L)\ge\frac1{4T}.$$ All constants preceding $m,n$ are uniform in their values and in the affine alphabets.
  - Explanation: Truth-table differentiation converts retained energy into a lower bound on face correlation.
- **C10** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-projected-row-atom)): Let $Q_x^Y$ select any collection of complete exact-mask/Hermite components with $\|I\|\le s$, using only $x,Y,I,S$. For a fixed axis $i$ let $q_{x,i}^Y$ be the maximum odd row coefficient of $Q_x^Yh_x$ in $r=(i,x_{-i})$, with the other rows and $c$ held fixed. Then $$\label{eq:projector-maximum} \mathbb E\mathbb E_{x\in Y}q_{x,i}^Y \le C_0\left(\mathbb E\mathbb E_{x\in X}a_{x,i}^2\right)^{1/2}, \qquad C_0=4^M\binom{N+s}{s}.$$ The estimate is valid for every weighted distribution of proof indices.
  - Explanation: Complete mask and Hermite projections transfer row-coefficient bounds without counting label frequencies.
- **C11** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-box-cs)): For every real array $v$, $$\label{eq:box-cs} \mathfrak d_Y(v)^{2^m} \le\mathbb E_{u^0,u^1}\prod_{\omega\in\{0,1\}^m}v(u^\omega).$$ In particular the right side is nonnegative.
  - Explanation: Iterated Cauchy–Schwarz removes arbitrary face signs and bounds their correlation by a cube product.
- **C12** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-base-equation-game)): If no assignment satisfies more than $3/4$ of the equation list, the ordinary equation-versus-variable game has value at most $11/12$. Consequently its ordinary $r$-fold repetition has value at most $c^r$, for the effective universal constant $c<1$ of Theorem <a href="#thm:parallel-repetition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:parallel-repetition">5</a>, for every $r\ge0$.
  - Explanation: A violated equation forces positional disagreement, supplying the base game value bound.
- **C13** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-hint-soundness)): Suppose no assignment satisfies more than $3/4$ of the equation list. For every $q\ge0$ and $t\ge t_0\ge1$, the value of the game with $q$ affine hints is at most $$\label{eq:hint-bound} \bigl(1-2^{-q}(1-c)\bigr)^{t_0}.$$ The bound also applies to strategies using arbitrary independent shared or private randomness. In particular, for fixed $q$ and any prescribed rational $s>0$, an effective choice of $t_0$ makes the bound strictly less than $s$, simultaneously for every $t\ge t_0$, every equation-list size, and every completeness parameter in Theorem <a href="#thm:max3lin" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:max3lin">4</a>.
  - Explanation: Zero-coefficient hidden coordinates retain repeated-game soundness even with shared affine hints.
- **C14** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-smoothness)): Fix any actual first question $U$, integers $d\ge1$ and $t\ge t_0\ge1$, and draw the remaining outer sample conditional on $U$. Draw $L_1,\ldots,L_d$ independently and uniformly from $D(A_V)$. Then $$\label{eq:smoothness-bound} \operatorname{TV}\left(\mathcal L\bigl((\pi_e^*L_1,\ldots,\pi_e^*L_d)\mid U\bigr), \operatorname{Unif}(D(A_U)^d)\right) \le\frac12\sqrt{(2^{4dt_0}-1)\frac{t_0^2}{t}}.$$ The bound is uniform in $U$ and the equation list, including repeated variable names and zero-dimensional local alphabets. For fixed $d,t_0$ and positive rational $\Delta$, an effective integer $t\ge t_0$ makes the right-hand side strictly less than $\Delta$.
  - Explanation: The whole collection of pulled-back forms approaches joint uniformity conditional on the first question.
- **C15** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-rem-joint-smoothness-use)): Because <a href="#eq:smoothness-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:smoothness-bound">[eq:smoothness-bound]</a> is uniform in $U$, it may be averaged over any distribution of $U$ and any extra randomness independent of the outer sample. In particular, if a bounded statistic is chosen as a function of $U$ and some of the pulled-back forms, one may apply Lemma <a href="#lem:smoothness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:smoothness">14</a> to the joint collection of all forms used by that statistic. For a statistic bounded in absolute value by $B$, the change in expectation is at most $2B$ times the displayed total-variation bound. This does not assert uniformity of one form conditional on previously revealed forms.
  - Explanation: Joint total variation controls background-dependent statistics without asserting conditional uniformity of an individual form.
- **C16** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-bit-gap)): For every fixed integer $J\ge20$, effective choices of constants give a four-test comparison system with the following properties. It is constructed from the uniform equation list in Theorem <a href="#thm:max3lin" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:max3lin">4</a>, for a fixed completeness error $\zeta>0$ chosen by the construction. Every test compares two bits in folded proof tables, and its contribution to cost is its failure probability divided by a positive rational budget $b_j$. In the YES case there are proof tables of cost at most $4$; in the NO case every collection of proof tables has cost greater than $J$. Alphabet sizes are bounded by effective constants depending only on $J$, and all local numerical parameters depend only on $J$.
  - Explanation: Composition separates honest and arbitrary proof-table comparison costs using constants fixed before the alphabet.
- **C17** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-finite-game)): There is a deterministic construction, with all preliminary work depending only on $K$, of a finite rational replacement for the four tests such that $$\label{eq:uniform-finite-error} \lvert\operatorname{Cost}_{\mathrm{fin}}(f)-\operatorname{Cost}_\infty(f)\rvert<\frac12 \qquad\text{for every collection of folded proof tables }f.$$ Consequently its minimum cost is less than $9/2$ in the YES case, and every proof has cost greater than $10K-1/2$ in the NO case. The rational distribution replacing $(T_0,T_1)$ has a positive integer common denominator depending only on $K$, computed by the construction.
  - Explanation: An explicitly enumerated rational sign-table law approximates every proof cost uniformly.
- **C18** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-graph-realization)): The finite rational comparison system can be transformed deterministically into a simple undirected unweighted graph $G$ with $$\label{eq:exact-graph-optimum} \operatorname{OPT}_{\mathrm{uncut}}(G) =D_0\min_f\operatorname{Cost}_{\mathrm{fin}}(f).$$ The graph has $O_K(M_0^t)$ vertices and edges and is constructed in polynomial time, including bit complexity, for fixed $K$.
  - Explanation: Fresh parity paths realize each failed signed demand with exact uncut cost and no additive baseline.

        "exact_quote": "Assume $\\varepsilon_1\\le Jb_1$ and $\\varepsilon_2\\le Jb_2$, and use <a href=\"#eq:smoothing-parameters\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:smoothing-parameters\">[eq:smoothing-parameters]</a>–<a href=\"#eq:hermite-cutoff-choice\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:hermite-cutoff-choice\">[eq:hermite-cutoff-choice]</a>.         "anchor": "stmt-lem-projected-row-atom",
        "exact_quote": "Let $Q_x^Y$ select any collection of complete exact-mask/Hermite components with $|I|\\le s$, using only $x,Y,I,S$. For a fixed axis $i$ let $q_{x,i}^Y$ be the maximum odd row coefficient of $Q_x^Yh_x$ in $r=(i,x_{-i})$, with the other rows and $c$ held fixed. Then $$\\label{eq:projector-maximum}\n \\mathbb E\\mathbb E_{x\\in Y}q_{x,i}^Y\n \\le C_0\\left(\\mathbb E\\mathbb E_{x\\in X}a_{x,i}^2\\right)^{1/2},\n \\qquad C_0=4^M\\binom{N+s}{s}.$$ The estimate is valid for every weighted distribution of proof indices.",
        "source_id": "PM-102.4"
      },

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-hint-soundness",
        "exact_quote": "Suppose no assignment satisfies more than $3/4$ of the equation list. For every $q\\ge0$ and $t\\ge t_0\\ge1$, the value of the game with $q$ affine hints is at most $$\\label{eq:hint-bound}\n \\bigl(1-2^{-q}(1-c)\\bigr)^{t_0}.$$ The bound also applies to strategies using arbitrary independent shared or private randomness.         "anchor": "stmt-lem-smoothness",
        "exact_quote": "Fix any actual first question $U$, integers $d\\ge1$ and $t\\ge t_0\\ge1$, and draw the remaining outer sample conditional on $U$. Draw $L_1,\\ldots,L_d$ independently and uniformly from $D(A_V)$. Then $$\\label{eq:smoothness-bound}\n \\operatorname{TV}\\left(\\mathcal L\\bigl((\\pi_e^*L_1,\\ldots,\\pi_e^*L_d)\\mid U\\bigr),\n                 \\operatorname{Unif}(D(A_U)^d)\\right)\n \\le\\frac12\\sqrt{(2^{4dt_0}-1)\\frac{t_0^2}{t}}.$$ The bound is uniform in $U$ and the equation list, including repeated variable names and zero-dimensional local alphabets.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-bit-gap",
        "exact_quote": "For every fixed integer $J\\ge20$, effective choices of constants give a four-test comparison system with the following properties. It is constructed from the uniform equation list in Theorem <a href=\"#thm:max3lin\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"thm:max3lin\">4</a>, for a fixed completeness error $\\zeta>0$ chosen by the construction. Every test compares two bits in folded proof tables, and its contribution to cost is its failure probability divided by a positive rational budget $b_j$. In the YES case there are proof tables of cost at most $4$; in the NO case every collection of proof tables has cost greater than $J$. Alphabet sizes are bounded by effective constants depending only on $J$, and all local numerical parameters depend only on $J$.",
        "source_id": "PM-102.4"
      },

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Uncut cost and optimum** ([source](#sec:introduction)): For a finite simple undirected graph $G=(V,E)$ and a bipartition $V=S\sqcup(V\setminus S)$, define $$\operatorname{uncut}_G(S)=\lvert\{uv\in E:u,v\in S\text{ or }u,v\notin S\}\rvert, \qquad \operatorname{OPT}_{\rm uncut}(G)=\min_{S\subseteq V}\operatorname{uncut}_G(S).$$
- **Affine function space** ([source](#pm-h-10)): For an affine space $A$, let $D(A)$ be the vector space of affine functions $A\to\mathbb F_2$, including constants.
- **Dual frequency and character** ([source](#pm-h-10)): A frequency is an element $\alpha$ of the linear dual $D(A)^*$, with character $\chi_\alpha(b)=(-1)^{\alpha(b)}$.
- **Odd frequency** ([source](#pm-h-10)): Call $\alpha$ *odd* if $\alpha(1)=1$.
- **Folded bit proof** ([source](#pm-h-14)): For a nonempty affine alphabet $A$, a *folded bit proof* is a function $$f:\{P:A\to\{1,-1\}\}\longrightarrow\{1,-1\}, \qquad f(-P)=-f(P).$$
- **Smoothed gradient field** ([source](#eq:smoothed-gradient)): For every folded proof define $$\label{eq:smoothed-gradient} F_B(c)=\mathbb E_{g,\lambda} f(P_{c+\sigma g,\eta\lambda}(B)), \qquad h_x(B,c)=\sqrt N\,\partial_{c_x}F_B(c).$$
- **Game value** ([source](#pm-h-12)): Its value is the maximum acceptance probability of separate deterministic response functions.
- **Canonical folded proof variables** ([source](#eq:finite-folding)): If $a_*$ is its first label, define $$\tau(P)=P(a_*),\qquad R(P)=\tau(P)P.$$

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R1** [claim](#stmt-thm-max3lin) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-max3lin): Uniformize explicit rational equation weights by largest-remainder rounding. -> Uniform nonempty equation list.
- **R2** [claim](#stmt-lem-base-equation-game) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-base-equation-game): Use violated equations to force positional disagreement. -> Base equation-game value bound.
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-lem-hint-soundness) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hint-soundness): Condition on coefficients and expose inactive coordinates; apply ordinary repetition. -> Hinted soundness bound.
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-lem-smoothness) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-smoothness): Compare mixed product densities and control intersections of hidden-coordinate sets. -> Joint marginal smoothness bound.
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-lem-smoothing-cutoffs) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-smoothing-cutoffs): Apply Gaussian energy identities and spectral row resampling. -> Low-degree retained gradient energy.
- **R6** [claim](#stmt-prop-correlation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-correlation): Differentiate truth-table coordinates and truncate coefficient mass. -> Expected face correlation lower bound.
- **R7** [claim](#stmt-thm-inner) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-box-cs-2): Partition complete masks, localize a random box, clip slices, and apply box Cauchy–Schwarz. -> Odd conditional row atom.
- **R8** [claim](#stmt-prop-bit-gap) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-bit-gap): Transfer row atoms through joint pullback and decode agreeing short label lists. -> Comparison cost gap.
- **R9** [claim](#stmt-lem-finite-game) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-finite-game): Truncate Gaussian coordinates, integrate a positive rational polynomial, and enumerate joint sign tables. -> Finite rational comparison law.
- **R10** [claim](#stmt-lem-graph-realization) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-graph-realization): Clear cost denominators and attach fresh parity paths. -> Simple unweighted graph with exact optimum correspondence.
- **R11** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-graph-realization-2): Choose the positive integer threshold and retain strict multiplicative slack. -> Effective polynomial-time graph gap reduction.
- **R12** [claim](#stmt-cor-min-2cnf-deletion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-graph-realization-3): Replace every edge by complementary clauses and compare false clauses with uncut edges. -> Clause-deletion hardness with preserved optimum.

With probability at least $p$, the function $$b\\longmapsto h_x(B[B^i_{x_{-i}}\\leftarrow b],c)$$ has a Fourier coefficient of absolute value at least $\\theta$ at an odd frequency of $D(A_\\xi)$. Equivalently, the event is $$\\label{eq:inner-atom-event}\n \\max_{\\alpha\\in D(A_\\xi)^*: \\alpha(1)=1}\n \\left|\\mathbb E_{b\\in D(A_\\xi)}\n h_x(B[B^i_{x_{-i}}\\leftarrow b],c)(-1)^{\\alpha(b)}\\right|\n \\ge\\theta.$$ For every folded proof, without any test assumption, one has pointwise $$\\label{eq:gradient-bounds}\n \\lVert h(B,c)\\rVert_{2,x}\\le\\sigma^{-1},\\qquad\n |h_x(B,c)|\\le\\sqrt N/\\sigma.$$",

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **I_PATH_COST**: For fixed endpoint signs, each fresh path gadget has minimum uncut cost equal to the failure indicator of its signed demand, including coincident endpoints.
- **I_FIXED_FACTOR**: The graph reduction has polynomial construction work and bit complexity when the requested approximation factor is fixed.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **A_FULL_REDUCTION**: Use the finite gadget implementation as a complete hardness reduction. -> The gadget requires the finite comparison system and integer multiplicities; it does not construct the preceding comparison gap.
- **A_BASELINE**: Replace the paths by a gadget with an additive cost baseline. -> An additive term does not preserve the exact minimum demand cost used by the multiplicative gap.
- **A_UNIFORM_K**: Claim a uniform efficient construction while varying the approximation factor with the source instance. -> Preliminary work is only bounded as a finite constant for a fixed approximation factor.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The fresh path realization is finite and constructive; the checked scope is its signed-demand cost identity.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON list of {n, demands}; demand = [u,v,relation,multiplicity].
# PRECONDITIONS: 1 <= n <= 3; valid endpoints; relation +/-1; nonnegative integer multiplicity; total <= 3.
# OUTPUTS: JSON list of conditional minimum uncut costs and their global minimum.
# TIME: O(sum over instances of 2**(n+3*total) * (n+4*total)).
# SPACE: O(n+4*total+2**n) per instance.
# ARITHMETIC: exact integers with python-flint fmpz.
# FAILURE: invalid input raises ValueError; exhaustive scope is bounded.
import json,sys
from flint import fmpz

def realize(case):
    n=case['n']; demands=case['demands']
    if type(n) is not int or not 1 <= n <= 3:
        raise ValueError('vertex bound')
    total=0
    for u,v,rel,mult in demands:
        if any(type(z) is not int for z in (u,v,rel,mult)) or not (0 <= u < n and 0 <= v < n and rel in (-1,1) and mult >= 0):
            raise ValueError('demand')
        total+=mult
    if total > 3: raise ValueError('multiplicity bound')
    edges=[]; count=n
    for u,v,rel,mult in demands:
        length = 4 if rel == 1 else 3
        for _ in range(mult):
            chain=[u]+list(range(count,count+length-1))+[v]
            count+=length-1
            edges.extend(zip(chain,chain[1:]))
    if any(a==b for a,b in edges) or len({tuple(sorted(e)) for e in edges}) != len(edges):
        raise ValueError('not simple')
    costs=[]
    for endpoints in range(1 << n):
        best=None
        for internals in range(1 << (count-n)):
            bits=endpoints | (internals << n)
            cost=sum((fmpz(1) for a,b in edges if ((bits >> a)&1) == ((bits >> b)&1)), fmpz(0))
            if best is None or cost < best: best=cost
        costs.append(int(best))
    return {'conditional':costs,'optimum':min(costs)}
print(json.dumps([realize(c) for c in json.loads(sys.stdin.read())],sort_keys=True,separators=(',',':')))
```

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | A graph optimisation component can use the hardness result to qualify a requested fixed-factor guarantee when its objective and feasible bipartitions match Min-UnCut. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The games are finite proof-verification objects with separate response functions. A contract for gameplay, physical simulation, or strategic learning is ABSENT. |
| tools | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | An exact graph-conversion checker can compare violated signed demands with uncut gadget cost while auditing simplicity and fresh internal vertices. |
| algorithms | True | strong | implementable | inferred | The signed-demand construction gives a finite deterministic transformation whose conditional graph cost equals the number of violated demands without an additive baseline. |
| data_structures | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | Canonical Boolean truth tables identify folded proof variables by question and complementary query pair; occurrence metadata must not split semantically identical queries. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The construction specifies exact finite enumeration and bit complexity but supplies no accelerator architecture, memory layout, or hardware execution contract: ABSENT. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The reduction supplies a worst-case approximation gap. A cryptographic primitive, distributional security assumption, or adversarial security contract is ABSENT. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Folded proof tables encode labels in a hardness proof. A training objective, generalisation guarantee, or statistical learning contract is ABSENT. |
| optimisation_operations_research | True | strong | adaptable | inferred | The hardness result constrains fixed-factor approximation guarantees for edge deletion to bipartiteness and transfers exactly to minimum clause deletion under the stated graph-to-formula construction. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Separate players model restricted information in proof verification. A distributed execution, communication, consistency, or fault-tolerance contract is ABSENT. |
| other (computational_complexity) | True | strong | adaptable | inferred | The source directly addresses computational complexity: an effective deterministic reduction establishes a strict approximation gap, with separate deterministic and randomized consequences. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L01** (hypothesis): The polynomial-time guarantee fixes the approximation factor before the input is given; effective constants may depend on that factor. Blocked use: ['Treating the construction as polynomial uniformly in a variable approximation factor.']
- **L02** (formal_scope): The target has explicit finite simple undirected unweighted graphs and a positive integer threshold. Transfer to a different objective requires a cost-preserving argument. Blocked use: ['Claiming the same result for directed cuts, vertex deletion, balanced cuts, or a different objective without a reduction.']
- **L03** (edge_case): The feasible bipartitions may have an empty part and need not be balanced. Blocked use: ['Imposing balance or nonempty parts while asserting the same optimum.']
- **L04** (approximation): A multiplicative Max-Cut approximation does not supply a comparable multiplicative Min-UnCut approximation near bipartiteness. Blocked use: ['Transferring a Max-Cut multiplicative guarantee by subtracting its reported cut size from the edge count.']
- **L05** (formal_scope): Deterministic and randomized approximation consequences are distinct: the randomized result gives the stated containment. Blocked use: ['Reporting the deterministic complexity consequence for a randomized approximation with constant success probability.']
- **L06** (hypothesis): A query and an affine hint are semantic functions encoded canonically, without hidden description metadata. Blocked use: ['Giving a decoder the originating equation, sampled position, or pullback description beyond its actual question and function.']
- **L07** (hypothesis): Repeated variable names do not identify answer slots across outer coordinates. Blocked use: ['Adding equality constraints between distinct fresh slots and applying the stated independence or repetition bounds.']
- **L08** (hypothesis): Joint smoothness controls the whole collection of pulled-back forms; it does not assert conditional uniformity after other forms have been revealed. Blocked use: ['Replacing a missing row by an independent uniform form after conditioning on a nonuniform shared background.']
- **L09** (edge_case): Coincident gadget endpoints represent self-demands, and all internal vertices remain fresh for each unit demand. Blocked use: ['Dropping a self-inequality as a loop, sharing internal vertices between demands, or adding a baseline cost.']
- **L10** (approximation): The finite replacement approximates the complete joint Gaussian sign tables rather than separate marginals. Blocked use: ['Independently sampling approximated marginal tables while claiming the same uniform comparison-cost bound.']
- **L11** (formal_scope): The hardness theorem is a worst-case reduction. A solver, practical running-time estimate, or certificate for a particular graph is ABSENT. Blocked use: ['Presenting finite gadget tests as a proof of the full hardness reduction or as an optimum certificate for an arbitrary input graph.']
- **L12** (formal_scope): Minimum clause deletion counts clause occurrences of unit cost. The displayed graph-to-formula mapping preserves this optimum. Blocked use: ['Silently deduplicating clauses or using weighted deletion costs while asserting the displayed optimum equality.']

### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 102 (`lean/docs/102.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [The Unique Games Theorem](../../preprints/The-Unique-Games-Theorem-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [A Direct Proof of Optimal Max-Cut Hardness](../../preprints/A-Direct-Proof-of-Optimal-Max-Cut-Hardness-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [The Factor-Two Hardness Threshold for Vertex Cover](../../preprints/The-Factor-Two-Hardness-Threshold-for-Vertex-Cover-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [Constant-factor hardness of Min-UnCut](../../preprints/Constant-factor-hardness-of-Min-UnCut-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [Constant-factor hardness of directed feedback vertex set](../../preprints/Constant-factor-hardness-of-directed-feedback-vertex-set-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The Unique Games conjecture asks for hardness of distinguishing nearly satisfiable unique games from games of very small value. The formalized result gives, for every fixed $0<\varepsilon,\delta<1/2$, a deterministic polynomial-time reduction from binary 3SAT to nonempty unweighted simple bipartite unique games. Satisfiable inputs have value at least $1-\varepsilon$ and unsatisfiable inputs have value at most $\delta$. The alphabet is a fixed $\mathbb F_2^s$ depending only on the errors, and every constraint is a translation.
>
> The formalized result establishes hardness of approximating Max-Cut beyond the Goemans–Williamson constant $\alpha_{\mathrm{GW}}$. For every fixed $\alpha_{\mathrm{GW}}<\alpha\le1$, it gives a deterministic polynomial-time reduction from binary 3SAT to a strictly separated Max-Cut gap on finite simple unweighted graphs. The reduction includes the explicit positive integer scaling used in the gap statement.
>
> The formalized result gives the factor-two hardness threshold for Vertex Cover. For each integer $m\ge4$, a polynomial-time reduction from binary 3SAT produces simple unweighted graphs with cover density below $1/2+1/m$ on satisfiable inputs and above $1-1/m$ on unsatisfiable inputs. Consequently, any polynomial-time approximation with a fixed factor $1\le\alpha<2$ would give a polynomial-time decision algorithm for 3SAT. No assumption that $P\ne NP$ is built into the statement.
>
> Min-UnCut minimizes the number of edges left uncut by a bipartition. The formalization constructs a deterministic reduction from encoded $3$-SAT formulas to finite simple unweighted Min-UnCut instances with arbitrarily large fixed multiplicative gaps. For every integer $K\ge2$, satisfiable formulas give optimum at most the output threshold, while unsatisfiable formulas give optimum strictly greater than $K$ times that threshold. Runtime and output length are polynomial for each fixed $K$, establishing hardness for every fixed approximation factor greater than one.
>
> A directed feedback vertex set meets every directed cycle. The formalization proves hardness of approximating the minimum such set within any fixed constant factor. For every real factor $A\ge1$ and every language in NP, it constructs a polynomial-time gap reduction to unweighted directed graphs with the stated completeness and soundness separation. Thus a fixed-factor polynomial-time approximation would imply a polynomial-time algorithm for every NP language.

Comparator source `MinUncut.lean` (4780 bytes, sha256 `92934869d5f570e8593a88eb8b6cb7ff0bc53876d4cdeda9c1e293283b9d9784`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

   
                                                                         
                                                                

                                                                          
                                                                         
                                                                        
                                                                      
  
namespace MinUncutGames.BinaryFormula

structure Literal where
  name : Nat
  positive : Bool
  deriving DecidableEq

def Literal.eval (literal : Literal) (assignment : Nat → Bool) : Bool :=
  if literal.positive then assignment literal.name else !(assignment literal.name)

abbrev Clause := Vector Literal 3

def Clause.eval (clause : Clause) (assignment : Nat → Bool) : Bool :=
  (clause[0].eval assignment || clause[1].eval assignment) || clause[2].eval assignment

structure Formula where
  clauses : List Clause

def Formula.Satisfiable (F : Formula) : Prop :=
  ∃ assignment : Nat → Bool, ∀ clause ∈ F.clauses, clause.eval assignment = true

end MinUncutGames.BinaryFormula

namespace MinUncutGames.BinaryEncoding
open BinaryFormula

def frame : List Bool → List Bool
  | [] => [false]
  | b :: bits => true :: b :: frame bits

def nameBits (name : Nat) : List Bool := frame name.bits

def literalBits (literal : Literal) : List Bool :=
  literal.positive :: nameBits literal.name

def clauseBits (clause : Clause) : List Bool :=
  literalBits clause[0] ++ literalBits clause[1] ++ literalBits clause[2]

def clausesBits : List Clause → List Bool
  | [] => [false]
  | clause :: clauses => true :: (clauseBits clause ++ clausesBits clauses)

def formulaBits (formula : Formula) : List Bool := clausesBits formula.clauses

end MinUncutGames.BinaryEncoding

namespace MinUncut
open MinUncutGames.BinaryFormula MinUncutGames.BinaryEncoding

                                                                        
                                                                           
structure Output where
  vertices : ℕ
  adjacent : Fin vertices → Fin vertices → Bool
  symmetric : ∀ u v, adjacent u v = adjacent v u
  loopless : ∀ u, adjacent u u = false
  threshold : ℕ
  threshold_pos : 1 ≤ threshold

                                                                              
                                                           
def Output.uncut (G : Output) (cut : Fin G.vertices → Bool) : ℕ :=
  (Finset.univ.filter fun uv : Fin G.vertices × Fin G.vertices =>
    uv.1 < uv.2 ∧ G.adjacent uv.1 uv.2 = true ∧ cut uv.1 = cut uv.2).card

                                                                             
                                     
noncomputable def Output.opt (G : Output) : ℕ :=
  (Finset.univ.image G.uncut).min' (by
    exact Finset.image_nonempty.mpr Finset.univ_nonempty)

                                                                        
                                                                           
def Output.bits (G : Output) : List Bool :=
  nameBits G.vertices ++
    ((List.finRange G.vertices).flatMap fun u =>
      (List.finRange G.vertices).map fun v => G.adjacent u v) ++
    nameBits G.threshold

                                                                               
                                                             
def inputBits (input : ℕ × Formula) : List Bool :=
  nameBits input.1 ++ formulaBits input.2

                                                                            
                                                                         
                                                                       
                                                                 
structure Reduction where
  reduce : ℕ → Formula → Output
  machine : Turing.TM2Computable inputBits Output.bits (fun p => reduce p.1 p.2)
  finiteAlphabet : ∀ k : machine.tm.K, Finite (machine.tm.Γ k)
  time : ℕ → Polynomial ℕ
  runtime : ∀ K φ,
    Turing.TM2OutputsInTime machine.tm
      ((inputBits (K, φ)).map machine.inputAlphabet.symm)
      (some (((reduce K φ).bits).map machine.outputAlphabet.symm))
      ((time K).eval (formulaBits φ).length)
  size : ℕ → Polynomial ℕ
  output_size : ∀ K φ,
    (reduce K φ).vertices + (reduce K φ).bits.length ≤
      (size K).eval (formulaBits φ).length
  yes : ∀ K, 2 ≤ K → ∀ φ, φ.Satisfiable →
    (reduce K φ).opt ≤ (reduce K φ).threshold
  no : ∀ K, 2 ≤ K → ∀ φ, ¬ φ.Satisfiable →
    K * (reduce K φ).threshold < (reduce K φ).opt

end MinUncut

namespace MinUncut

theorem main : Nonempty Reduction := by
  sorry

end MinUncut

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


For every fixed <i>C</i> &gt; 1, approximating Min-UnCut within factor <i>C</i> is NP-hard, even on simple undirected unweighted graphs.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

For a finite simple undirected graph $G=(V,E)$ and a bipartition $V=S\sqcup(V\setminus S)$, define $$\operatorname{uncut}_G(S)=\lvert\{uv\in E:u,v\in S\text{ or }u,v\notin S\}\rvert,
 \qquad
 \operatorname{OPT}_{\rm uncut}(G)=\min_{S\subseteq V}\operatorname{uncut}_G(S).$$ The two parts may be empty and need not be balanced. Equivalently, $\operatorname{OPT}_{\rm uncut}(G)$ is the minimum number of edges whose deletion makes $G$ bipartite: deleting the uncut edges of a partition suffices, and a bipartition of any remaining bipartite graph makes every uncut edge belong to the deleted set. This is the edge-deletion problem throughout.

Min-UnCut measures distance from bipartiteness. Although its objective is the number of edges minus the maximum cut size, a multiplicative approximation for Max-Cut does not give a comparable multiplicative approximation for Min-UnCut when the graph is nearly bipartite. Its approximation question therefore concerns how accurately one can recognize and exploit that regime. Agarwal, Charikar, Makarychev and Makarychev gave a randomized polynomial-time $O(\sqrt{\log |V|})$ approximation for Min-UnCut (Agarwal et al. 2005, Theorem 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[ACMM2005](#ref-ACMM2005)]. Under the Unique Games Conjecture, the near-satisfiable Max-Cut gap of Khot, Kindler, Mossel and O’Donnell implies arbitrarily large constant gaps for weighted Min-UnCut (Khot et al. 2007) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[KKMO2007](#ref-KKMO2007)]: graphs admitting a cut that misses at most an $\eta$ fraction of the total edge weight are hard to distinguish from graphs in which every cut misses a constant times $\sqrt\eta$ of that weight. The ratio of these two uncut costs grows without bound as $\eta$ decreases.

Unconditional lower bounds for particular constants have developed through successively stronger finite gadgets. Håstad, Huang, Manokaran, O’Donnell and Wright proved hardness for every factor below $11/8$ in the weighted minimum-deletion formulation of Max-Cut (Håstad et al. 2017, Corollary 3.3) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[HHMOW2017](#ref-HHMOW2017)]. Wiman strengthened the near-satisfiable Max-Cut gap to give factors below $1.45685$ (Wiman 2018, Theorem 3.18) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Wiman2018](#ref-Wiman2018)]. Martinsson subsequently obtained hardness for every factor below $73139148/49096883\approx1.48969$ for minimizing the weight of violated two-variable parity equations (Martinsson 2024, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Martinsson2024](#ref-Martinsson2024)]. The last result transfers to weighted Min-UnCut by replacing equality and inequality constraints by paths of lengths four and three, respectively, with fresh internal vertices and the constraint’s weight on each edge. For fixed endpoint bits each path has minimum uncut weight equal to the violated constraint’s weight, so this preserves absolute minimum cost even though the total weight changes. These results concern individual constant factors; the theorem below gives every prescribed constant factor on simple unweighted graphs.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *For every fixed integer $K\ge2$, there is a deterministic polynomial-time many-one reduction mapping a $3$SAT formula $\varphi$ to a pair $(G,k)$, where $G$ is an explicit finite simple undirected unweighted graph and $k\ge1$ is a binary-encoded integer, such that $$\begin{aligned}
 \varphi\text{ satisfiable}&\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
       \operatorname{OPT}_{\rm uncut}(G)\le k,\\
 \varphi\text{ unsatisfiable}&\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
       \operatorname{OPT}_{\rm uncut}(G)>Kk.
 \end{aligned}$$ For each fixed $K$, the graph size, full construction work, and bit complexity are polynomial in the bit length of $\varphi$. All constants are effective; no advice or oracle is supplied to the reduction.*

</div>

Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> resolves the constant-factor approximability question for Min-UnCut in the hardness direction. It assumes neither the Unique Games Conjecture nor any other unproved hardness hypothesis. Choosing an integer $K$ at least any prescribed real factor $C>1$ shows that a deterministic polynomial-time $C$-approximation would imply $\mathsf P=\mathsf{NP}$. The positive threshold $k$ separates this assertion from ordinary bipartiteness testing. For a randomized approximation with constant success probability, the corresponding consequence is $\mathsf{NP}\subseteq\mathsf{RP}$; Section <a href="#sec:finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:finite">7</a> explains the one-sided test.

In minimum $2$CNF clause deletion, the input is an explicit list of two-literal disjunctions, each of unit cost, and one deletes as few clause occurrences as possible to make the remaining formula satisfiable. Write $\operatorname{OPT}_{\rm del}(F)$ for this minimum and $\operatorname{unsat}(F,a)$ for the number of clause occurrences falsified by an assignment $a$. Agarwal et al. also give a randomized polynomial-time $O(\sqrt{\log n})$ approximation for this problem on $n$ variables (Agarwal et al. 2005, Theorem 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[ACMM2005](#ref-ACMM2005)].

<a id="stmt-cor-min-2cnf-deletion"></a>
<div id="cor:min-2cnf-deletion" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2**. *For every fixed real $C>1$, minimum $2$CNF clause deletion is NP-hard to approximate within factor $C$. In particular, a deterministic polynomial-time $C$-approximation would imply $\mathsf P=\mathsf{NP}$.*

</div>

The proof in Section <a href="#sec:2cnf-consequence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:2cnf-consequence">7.5</a> replaces each edge by two clauses and preserves the optimum deletion cost exactly.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Proof strategy

The reduction first constructs weighted comparisons between bits using Gaussian-sampled queries. A satisfying source instance admits bits of total comparison cost at most $4$, whereas an unsatisfiable instance forces cost greater than an arbitrarily prescribed constant $J$. The graph construction then realizes each failed comparison by exactly one uncut edge. The main work is obtaining this comparison gap with constants chosen before the label alphabet.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
###### A game with affine labels.

The starting point is Håstad’s near-satisfiable gap for three-variable parity equations (Håstad 2001) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Hastad2001](#ref-Hastad2001)], recalled with ordinary parallel repetition in Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a>. A first player receives a tuple of equations and labels it by satisfying assignments to the separate equations. A second player sees the full equations in most coordinates and only a queried variable in a small set of hidden coordinates. Its label supplies the corresponding bits. The verifier checks agreement. The two label sets are finite affine spaces over $\mathbb F_2$, and agreement is an affine map from the first space to the second.

Section <a href="#sec:outer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:outer">5</a> proves two properties of this game. Its soundness survives giving both players a fixed number of shared affine functions on their labels: many hidden coordinates have zero coefficients in all those functions and still support ordinary repetition. Also, a fixed number of uniform affine functions on the second alphabet, pulled back through the random agreement map, have joint distribution close to independent uniform functions on the first alphabet. The latter is a statement about the whole collection; it allows later decoding choices to depend on some of its members. These properties are proved directly for the questions used here.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
###### A comparison test for arbitrary bit proofs.

At each question with alphabet $A$, replace a label $a$ by a function $f$ assigning a sign $f(P)\in\{1,-1\}$ to every Boolean query $P:A\to\{1,-1\}$. The intended function evaluates queries, $f(P)=P(a)$. Arbitrary functions are required only to satisfy $f(-P)=-f(P)$, the usual folding relation. We must extract labels from arbitrary such functions whose comparison cost is small.

Section <a href="#sec:inner-smoothing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:inner-smoothing">3</a> constructs three tests using random affine functions and Gaussian thresholds. The affine functions are arranged on $X=[n]^m$ as sums of $m$ arrays, with the $i$th array independent of the $i$th coordinate. Each test compares two queries obtained by a controlled change of this data. The tests respectively control Gaussian perturbations, resampling of array entries, and changes in individual entries of a Boolean truth table. Evaluation proofs have small cost in all three tests.

For a general proof, Gaussian averaging gives a smooth real-valued function of the threshold parameters. Its gradient retains nonzero energy because the first test rarely changes the answer. The other two tests control its Fourier degrees and its correlation with products of functions each missing one coordinate of $X$. These bounds use the number of array positions, not the number of affine labels.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
###### Extracting a label without an alphabet-size loss.

Section <a href="#sec:inner-box" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:inner-box">4</a> converts that correlation into a large Fourier coefficient in one affine-function entry, with all other entries fixed. The coefficient is odd: its character changes sign upon adding the constant function $1$. Each such character has the form $b\mapsto(-1)^{b(a)}$ for a unique label $a$, by Lemma <a href="#lem:odd-labels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:odd-labels">3</a>.

The extraction uses symmetries of the array representation to pair equal odd frequencies. Restricting to a random smaller box then separates their remaining random variables. Repeated Cauchy–Schwarz removes the arbitrary functions missing coordinates and leaves products over cube vertices. The terms involving the Gaussian coordinate at the tested point vanish by centering, while the paired terms factor over independent four-corner squares, each controlled by a Fourier coefficient. The selection of terms depends only on which array entries and Gaussian degrees occur; it never enumerates label frequencies. This distinction gives an extraction probability and coefficient size independent of the affine alphabet.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
###### Composition and finite graphs.

Section <a href="#sec:composition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:composition">6</a> adds a fourth test comparing the two players’ answers to the same pulled-back query. Low cost makes their smoothed one-entry functions close. The large coefficients on each side form short label lists. Joint approximation of the affine-function distributions controls the remaining small coefficients through a fourth-moment identity, even when the first list was chosen using shared background functions. The lists yield an agreeing pair with fixed positive probability, contradicting the game’s soundness. All inner constants are fixed before the game’s repetition parameters and alphabet.

Finally, Section <a href="#sec:finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:finite">7</a> approximates the complete joint Gaussian sign tables by a rational law. Enumerating this law gives a deterministic comparison system with polynomial numerical multiplicities for fixed $K$. Fresh paths of lengths four and three realize equality and inequality demands exactly, including demands with coincident endpoints. Their costs have no additive baseline, so a positive integer threshold preserves the strict multiplicative gap. The constants may be large; the approximation factor is fixed before the input formula is given.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Relation to earlier methods

The parity-equation starting point belongs to the PCP approach to hardness of approximation: a locally checked proof gives a finite constraint system with a gap between satisfiable and unsatisfiable inputs. Håstad’s construction provides the near-satisfiable parity gap used here, while the parallel repetition theorems of Raz and Holenstein amplify the corresponding two-player game (Håstad 2001; Raz 1998; Holenstein 2009) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Hastad2001](#ref-Hastad2001), [Raz1998](#ref-Raz1998), [Holenstein2009](#ref-Holenstein2009)]. Neither step assumes Unique Games. In particular, the completeness error is positive and is selected only after the repetitions have been fixed.

Our bit proofs use the long code and folding of Bellare, Goldreich and Sudan (Bellare, Goldreich, and Sudan 1998) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[BGS1998](#ref-BGS1998)], which also underlie Håstad’s Fourier analysis. Khot and Safra use smooth equation–variable games and decode short label lists from large Fourier coefficients (Khot and Safra 2013, secs. 3.2–3.4 and 5.2) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[KS13](#ref-KS13)]. Shared linear advice and coordinates on which that advice vanishes appear in Khot, Minzer and Safra (Khot, Minzer, and Safra 2025, secs. 3.3–3.4) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[KMS17](#ref-KMS17)]; a related shared-subspace formulation appears in Dinur et al. (Dinur et al. 2025, Lemma 5.4) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[DKKMS2025](#ref-DKKMS2025)]. Our outer-game proofs retain this mechanism but establish the precise hinted soundness and joint approximation estimates needed by a decoder that depends on its shared background.

The analytic tools also have established precedents. The Gaussian sign identity is the random-hyperplane calculation of Goemans and Williamson (Goemans and Williamson 1995) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[GW95](#ref-GW95)], and Gaussian smoothing and Hermite expansions are standard in noise-stability analysis (Mossel, O’Donnell, and Oleszkiewicz 2010) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[MOO10](#ref-MOO10)]. The box estimate uses the iterated Cauchy–Schwarz mechanism in Gowers’s hypergraph arguments (Gowers 2007) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Gowers2007](#ref-Gowers2007)]. We prove the identities and inequalities used here. The additional argument is the extraction of an odd one-entry coefficient from an arbitrary bit proof, uniformly in its alphabet, and its transfer through a random affine map when the remaining coefficients depend on the background.

<a id="sec:preliminaries"></a>
### Preliminaries and unconditional starting theorems

All finite-set averages and Fourier transforms use probability normalization. Every affine space in the paper is a finite nonempty affine space over $\mathbb F_2$. Constants described as fixed are independent of the input instance; their dependence on the requested approximation factor is allowed.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Affine alphabets and odd characters

For an affine space $A$, let $D(A)$ be the vector space of affine functions $A\to\mathbb F_2$, including constants. If $A$ has dimension $r$, then $D(A)$ has dimension $r+1$. Write $1\in D(A)$ for the constant-one function. A frequency is an element $\alpha$ of the linear dual $D(A)^*$, with character $\chi_\alpha(b)=(-1)^{\alpha(b)}$. Call $\alpha$ *odd* if $\alpha(1)=1$.

<a id="stmt-lem-odd-labels"></a>
<div id="lem:odd-labels" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (Odd frequencies are labels). *The map $a\mapsto\operatorname{ev}_a$, where $\operatorname{ev}_a(b)=b(a)$, is a bijection from $A$ onto the odd frequencies of $D(A)$. If $\pi:A\to A'$ is affine and $\pi^*:D(A')\to D(A)$ is pullback, then its dual sends $\operatorname{ev}_a$ to $\operatorname{ev}_{\pi(a)}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-odd-labels"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-odd-labels</summary>

*Proof.* Choose affine coordinates $A=\mathbb F_2^r$. Every affine function is $b(x)=b_0+\sum_{j=1}^r b_jx_j$. A functional taking the constant function to one is uniquely $b\mapsto b_0+\sum_j b_ja_j$, for some $a\in\mathbb F_2^r$. Finally $(\pi^*)^*(\operatorname{ev}_a)(b)=b(\pi(a))$. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:fourth-moment"></a>
For a real function $v$ on a finite binary vector space $W$, write $$\widehat v(\alpha)=\mathbb E_{w\in W}v(w)(-1)^{\alpha(w)},\qquad
 v(w)=\sum_{\alpha\in W^*}\widehat v(\alpha)(-1)^{\alpha(w)}.$$ Character orthogonality gives Parseval’s identity $\sum_\alpha\lvert\widehat v(\alpha)\rvert^2=\mathbb Ev^2$. If $T:W'\to W$ is linear, each character of $v$ pulls back to its image under $T^*$; coefficients with the same image add. Injectivity and surjectivity of $T$ are unnecessary. We will use the identity $$\label{eq:fourth-moment}
 \sum_\alpha\lvert\widehat v(\alpha)\rvert^4
 =\mathbb E_{a,b,c\in W}v(a)v(b)v(c)v(a+b+c).$$ Indeed, expansion of the four factors and averaging over the three independent points force all four frequencies to agree.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Gaussian identities

We recall the Fourier–Hermite identities used in Gaussian noise-stability analysis (Mossel, O’Donnell, and Oleszkiewicz 2010, sec. 4.1 and 5) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[MOO10](#ref-MOO10)], with proofs of the needed forms. Let $\gamma_d$ be standard Gaussian probability measure on $\mathbb R^d$. The normalized Hermite polynomials $H_j$ are specified by $$e^{tz-t^2/2}=\sum_{j\ge0}H_j(z)\frac{t^j}{\sqrt{j!}}.$$ Gaussian integration of the product of two generating functions proves their orthonormality. Their products $\psi_I(c)=\prod_x H_{I_x}(c_x)$ form an orthonormal basis of $L^2(\gamma_d)$. For completeness, if a function is orthogonal to all these polynomials, its Gaussian-weighted exponential transform has every Taylor coefficient zero. Cauchy–Schwarz makes that transform entire in each variable; its restriction to imaginary arguments is the Fourier transform of an integrable function, which is therefore zero. Thus the original function is zero almost everywhere.

<a id="eq:hermite-energy"></a>
Differentiating the generating function gives $H_j'=\sqrt j H_{j-1}$. Consequently, for a function with square-integrable weak gradient, $$\label{eq:hermite-energy}
 \int\lVert\nabla F\rVert^2\,\mathrm d\gamma_d
 =\sum_I |I|\lvert\widehat F(I)\rvert^2,
 \qquad |I|=\sum_x I_x.$$ One first proves this for polynomials, and then passes to the Hermite partial sums and weak derivatives. In the smoothed functions used below the weighted series converges directly. In particular, mean-zero $F$ satisfies the Gaussian Poincaré inequality $\int F^2\,\mathrm d\gamma_d\le\int\lVert\nabla F\rVert^2\,\mathrm d\gamma_d$.

<a id="eq:gaussian-derivative"></a>
For $0<\rho<1$, the Gaussian noise operator $$T_\rho Q(c)=\mathbb E_g Q(\rho c+\sqrt{1-\rho^2}\,g)$$ multiplies $\psi_I$ by $\rho^{|I|}$, as follows by averaging its generating function. If $Q$ is bounded and $\sigma>0$, ordinary Gaussian convolution is smooth and $$\label{eq:gaussian-derivative}
 \partial_{c_x}\mathbb E_g Q(c+\sigma g)
 =\frac1\sigma\mathbb E_g g_xQ(c+\sigma g).$$ Differentiation of the Gaussian density is justified by its integrable derivatives; an additional independent average may be included in $Q$.

<a id="eq:gaussian-signs"></a>
We also use the elementary sign identity underlying random-hyperplane rounding (Goemans and Williamson 1995, Lemma 3.2) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[GW95](#ref-GW95)]: if $u,v$ are unit vectors and $g$ is standard isotropic Gaussian, then $$\label{eq:gaussian-signs}
 \mathbb P[\operatorname{sgn}\langle g,u\rangle\ne\operatorname{sgn}\langle g,v\rangle]
 =\frac{\angle(u,v)}\pi\le\lVert u-v\rVert.$$ It follows by rotational symmetry in the two-dimensional plane spanned by $u,v$; the collinear cases follow by continuity. In particular, adding an independent centered Gaussian of standard deviation $a$ to a standard Gaussian changes its sign with probability $\arctan(a)/\pi\le a$.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### The starting gap and repetition

<a id="stmt-thm-max3lin"></a>
<div id="thm:max3lin" class="theorem">

**Theorem 4** (An unconditional equation gap). *For every fixed rational $\zeta>0$, there is a deterministic polynomial-time reduction from $3$SAT to a nonempty explicit list of equations $x_i+x_j+x_k=b$ over $\mathbb F_2$, with uniform sampling from the list, such that satisfiable inputs admit an assignment satisfying at least $1-\zeta$ of the list and unsatisfiable inputs admit none satisfying more than $3/4$. The list length and the full bit complexity are polynomial for fixed $\zeta$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-max3lin"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-max3lin</summary>

*Proof.* Håstad’s Theorem 5.4, including the verifier-to-equations construction in its proof, gives a deterministic polynomial construction of rationally weighted three-variable equations with YES value at least $1-\delta$ and NO value at most $(1+\delta)/2$, for every fixed sufficiently small dyadic $\delta>0$ (Håstad 2001) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Hastad2001](#ref-Hastad2001)]. Here the weights and their polynomial-size support are explicit.

We record why a uniform list costs only polynomial work. For a probability vector $(w_1,\ldots,w_r)$ of rational weights and an integer $Q$, take $\lfloor Qw_j\rfloor$ copies of equation $j$ and distribute the remaining copies among the largest fractional remainders. The resulting probabilities differ from the original ones in total variation by at most $r/(2Q)$. All floors, comparisons and sorting require polynomial bit complexity. Choose a fixed $\varepsilon>0$ and $\delta>0$ with $\delta+\varepsilon<\zeta$ and $(1+\delta)/2+\varepsilon<3/4$, then $Q\ge r/(2\varepsilon)$ with $Q=O_\varepsilon(r)$. Satisfaction probabilities change by at most $\varepsilon$ for every assignment simultaneously, giving the asserted list. The underlying construction can be taken nonempty; any exceptional bounded input cases are decidable directly and replaced by fixed YES or NO lists. ◻

</details>

A finite two-player game consists of a joint distribution on questions $(U,V)$, finite answer sets, and an acceptance predicate. Its value is the maximum acceptance probability of separate deterministic response functions. Independent shared or private randomness does not increase this maximum: fixing the random seed realizes at least its average success. The ordinary $r$-fold repetition samples $r$ independent question pairs and accepts only if all coordinates accept.

<a id="stmt-thm-parallel-repetition"></a>
<div id="thm:parallel-repetition" class="theorem">

**Theorem 5** (Uniform parallel repetition). *There is an effectively specified constant $c<1$ such that every finite game with at most eight first-player answers, at most two second-player answers, and value at most $11/12$ has $r$-fold repeated value at most $c^r$ for all integers $r\ge0$. The same $c$ works for all question distributions and all question-set sizes.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-parallel-repetition"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-parallel-repetition</summary>

*Proof.* This is an instance of ordinary parallel repetition (Raz 1998) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Raz1998](#ref-Raz1998)]. More explicitly, Theorem 2.5 of Holenstein (Holenstein 2009) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Holenstein2009](#ref-Holenstein2009)], after padding the answer sets to sizes eight and two, permits $$c=\left(1-\frac1{6000\cdot12^3}\right)^{1/4}<1.$$ Holenstein’s Theorem 2.5 imposes no regularity condition on the joint question distribution. A rational upper bound strictly between this $c$ and one may be used in every effective parameter choice below. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:inner-smoothing"></a>
### The bit test and its smoothed correlation

We first define the inner test, including the distribution of its queries. Its soundness statement is uniform in the size of the affine alphabet. This uniformity will permit us to select the inner parameters before the outer repetition parameters.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Folded proofs, face arrays, and queries

For a nonempty affine alphabet $A$, a *folded bit proof* is a function $$f:\{P:A\to\{1,-1\}\}\longrightarrow\{1,-1\},
 \qquad f(-P)=-f(P).$$ A query is the Boolean function $P$ itself: two descriptions that give the same function on $A$ must receive the same answer. The intended proof at $a\in A$ is evaluation, $f(P)=P(a)$. The evaluation encoding is the long code of the label, and the displayed antisymmetry is the standard folding relation (Bellare, Goldreich, and Sudan 1998; Håstad 2001) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[BGS1998](#ref-BGS1998), [Hastad2001](#ref-Hastad2001)]. Nonemptiness of $A$ ensures that $P\ne-P$, so the folding requirement is consistent.

We allow a finite random index $\xi$ with arbitrary probability weights to select an alphabet $A_\xi$ and a folded proof $f_\xi$. Conditional on $\xi$, all sampling below is performed on its indicated alphabet. Expectations include $\xi$ unless a conditioning is stated. Thus a bound on the average test failure will not be replaced by a bound for every individual index. We usually suppress $\xi$ and write $A,f$.

<a id="eq:face-code"></a>
Let $m,n\ge2$ be integers, to be selected later. Write $$X=[n]^m,\qquad N=n^m,\qquad
 \mathcal R=\{(i,y):i\in[m],\ y\in[n]^{[m]\setminus\{i\}}\},
 \qquad M=|\mathcal R|=mn^{m-1}.$$ Here $[n]^S$ denotes coordinate tuples indexed by $S$. If $x\in X$, then $x_{-i}$ is the tuple obtained by deleting coordinate $i$, and $x_{-ij}$ deletes both $i$ and $j$. A *row* is one entry of an array $(B^i_y)_{(i,y)\in\mathcal R}$. We take these entries independently and uniformly from $D(A)$ and put <a id="eq:face-array"></a>$$\label{eq:face-array}
 B(x)=\sum_{i=1}^m B^i_{x_{-i}}\in D(A),
 \qquad B(a)=(B(x)(a))_{x\in X}.$$ All sums of affine functions or binary arrays are over $\mathbb F_2$. The *face code* is the subspace $$\label{eq:face-code}
 \mathcal C=\left\{z\in\mathbb F_2^X:
 z_x=\sum_{i=1}^m z^i_{x_{-i}}
 \text{ for some }z^i\in\mathbb F_2^{[n]^{[m]\setminus\{i\}}}\right\}.$$ In particular, $B(a)\in\mathcal C$ for every $a\in A$. The code depends only on $m,n$, even when the index $\xi$ changes the alphabet.

For $u\in\mathbb R^X$ and $d\in\mathbb R^{\mathcal C}$ define a table on $\mathcal C$ by <a id="eq:inner-query"></a>$$\label{eq:inner-query}
 P_{u,d}(z)=\operatorname{sgn}\left(\frac1{\sqrt N}
                  \sum_{x\in X}u_x(-1)^{z_x}+d_z\right).$$ Use a fixed convention at zero. Its pullback is the Boolean query $P_{u,d}(B):a\mapsto P_{u,d}(B(a))$. If $P$ is a table on $\mathcal C$ and $z\in\mathcal C$, write $P^{z,+}$ and $P^{z,-}$ for the same table with its entry at $z$ forced to $1$ and $-1$, respectively. Repeated codewords among the labels present no difficulty: this operation changes the query value at every label with that codeword.

Let $0<\eta\le\sigma<1$ and $0<\delta<1$. Draw independent standard Gaussian vectors $c,g\in\mathbb R^X$ and $\lambda\in\mathbb R^{\mathcal C}$, independently of $\xi,B$, and set $$P=P_{c+\sigma g,\eta\lambda}.$$ The following tests fail precisely when the two indicated proof answers are unequal. A denominator is a positive weight normalization; it need not itself be a failure probability.

1.  Compare $f(P_{c,0}(B))$ and $f(P(B))$, with denominator $b_1=10\sigma$.

2.  Independently for each row, replace $B^i_y$ by a fresh uniform element of $D(A)$ with probability $\delta^2/m$, obtaining $B'$. Compare $f(P(B))$ and $f(P(B'))$, using the same $c,g,\lambda$ and the same table $P$ on both sides. The denominator is $b_2=10(\delta+\eta)$.

3.  Draw $z$ uniformly from $\mathcal C$, independently of the other random choices, and compare $f(P^{z,+}(B))$ and $f(P^{z,-}(B))$. The denominator is $b_3=|\mathcal C|^{-1}$.

Write $\varepsilon_j$ for the failure probability of test <span class="upright">(T$j$)</span>. Although the tables are defined at ties, every unforced threshold here has a continuous Gaussian input of positive variance, so the choice at ties does not affect any of these probabilities.

<a id="stmt-lem-honest"></a>
<div id="lem:honest" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (Honest proof budgets). *For any choice of labels $a_\xi\in A_\xi$, the evaluation proofs $f_\xi(P)=P(a_\xi)$ satisfy $\varepsilon_j\le b_j$ for $j=1,2,3$. In fact, $$\varepsilon_1\le 2\sigma,\qquad
 \varepsilon_2\le2\delta+2\eta,\qquad
 \varepsilon_3=|\mathcal C|^{-1}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-honest"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-honest</summary>

*Proof.* Condition on the index and its label $a$. For any $z\in\mathcal C$, put $$v_z=N^{-1/2}\bigl((-1)^{z_x}\bigr)_{x\in X};
 \qquad \lVert v_z\rVert_{\mathbb R^X}=1.$$ For the first test, conditional on $B$, the input $\langle c,v_{B(a)}\rangle$ is standard Gaussian, and its added perturbation $\sigma\langle g,v_{B(a)}\rangle+\eta\lambda_{B(a)}$ is an independent Gaussian of standard deviation $\sqrt{\sigma^2+\eta^2}$. The sign identity <a href="#eq:gaussian-signs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gaussian-signs">[eq:gaussian-signs]</a> gives disagreement probability $$\frac1\pi\arctan\sqrt{\sigma^2+\eta^2}
 \le\sqrt{\sigma^2+\eta^2}\le2\sigma.$$

For the second test, let $D$ be the relative Hamming distance between $B(a)$ and $B'(a)$. For each $x$, the evaluation $B(x)(a)$ can change only if at least one of its $m$ rows was selected for resampling. A union bound therefore gives $\mathbb ED\le\delta^2$. Consequently $$\mathbb E\lVert v_{B(a)}-v_{B'(a)}\rVert_{\mathbb R^X}
 =2\mathbb E\sqrt D\le2\delta.$$ Conditional on $B,B'$, apply <a href="#eq:gaussian-signs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gaussian-signs">[eq:gaussian-signs]</a> to the isotropic Gaussian $c+\sigma g$, initially omitting the table perturbations. Its two signs disagree with probability at most $\lVert v_{B(a)}-v_{B'(a)}\rVert$. Restoring each perturbation $\eta\lambda_{B(a)}$ or $\eta\lambda_{B'(a)}$ changes the corresponding sign with probability at most $\eta/\sqrt{1+\sigma^2}\le\eta$. The union bound gives $\varepsilon_2\le2\delta+2\eta$. Independence of the two table perturbations is not needed, so this reasoning also covers $B(a)=B'(a)$.

In the third test, the two evaluation answers differ if and only if $z=B(a)$. Uniform sampling of $z$ gives exactly $|\mathcal C|^{-1}$. All bounds hold conditional on the chosen label and can be averaged over $\xi$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### The decoding statement

<a id="eq:smoothed-gradient"></a>
For every folded proof define $$\label{eq:smoothed-gradient}
 F_B(c)=\mathbb E_{g,\lambda} f(P_{c+\sigma g,\eta\lambda}(B)),
 \qquad h_x(B,c)=\sqrt N\,\partial_{c_x}F_B(c).$$ These derivatives exist everywhere by Gaussian convolution, as also verified below. For real arrays on $X$ our conventions are $$\langle v,w\rangle_x=\frac1N\sum_{x\in X}v_xw_x,
 \qquad \lVert v\rVert_{2,x}^2=\frac1N\sum_{x\in X}|v_x|^2.$$ If a row $(i,x_{-i})$ is removed, the remaining rows are its *background*. For any background write $B[B^i_{x_{-i}}\leftarrow b]$ for the array with that row filled by $b\in D(A)$.

<a id="stmt-thm-inner"></a>
<div id="thm:inner" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:inner-atom-event"></a>
<a id="eq:gradient-bounds"></a>
**Theorem 7** (Inner decoding). *For every rational $J\ge1$, there are effectively selectable integers $m,n\ge2$ and positive rational constants $\sigma,\delta,\eta,p,\theta<1$, with $\eta\le\sigma$, depending only on $J$, with the following property. For any finite weighted family of nonempty affine alphabets and folded bit proofs, suppose $$\varepsilon_j\le Jb_j\qquad(j=1,2,3).$$ Draw $\xi$, a standard Gaussian $c\in\mathbb R^X$, independent uniform $x\in X$ and $i\in[m]$, and all background rows independently and uniformly in $D(A_\xi)$. With probability at least $p$, the function $$b\longmapsto h_x(B[B^i_{x_{-i}}\leftarrow b],c)$$ has a Fourier coefficient of absolute value at least $\theta$ at an odd frequency of $D(A_\xi)$. Equivalently, the event is $$\label{eq:inner-atom-event}
 \max_{\alpha\in D(A_\xi)^*: \alpha(1)=1}
 \left|\mathbb E_{b\in D(A_\xi)}
 h_x(B[B^i_{x_{-i}}\leftarrow b],c)(-1)^{\alpha(b)}\right|
 \ge\theta.$$ For every folded proof, without any test assumption, one has pointwise $$\label{eq:gradient-bounds}
 \lVert h(B,c)\rVert_{2,x}\le\sigma^{-1},\qquad
 |h_x(B,c)|\le\sqrt N/\sigma.$$*

</div>

The rest of this Section proves a low-degree correlation estimate. Section <a href="#sec:inner-box" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:inner-box">4</a> turns that estimate into <a href="#eq:inner-atom-event" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:inner-atom-event">[eq:inner-atom-event]</a>; the parameter choices in Section <a href="#sec:inner-completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:inner-completion">4.6</a> complete the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:inner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:inner">7</a>.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Quantitative smoothing and degree projections

<a id="eq:smoothing-parameters"></a>
<a id="eq:hermite-cutoff-choice"></a>
For the rest of this Section fix the following parameters, before choosing $m,n$: $$\label{eq:smoothing-parameters}
 \sigma=\frac1{2560J},\qquad
 \delta=\eta=\frac{\sigma^2}{2560J},\qquad
 R=\lceil\delta^{-2}\rceil,\qquad
 T=\left\lceil\frac{32J}{\eta\sigma^4}\right\rceil.$$ Choose an integer $s\ge1$ such that $$\label{eq:hermite-cutoff-choice}
 s+2>\sigma^{-2},\qquad
 (s+2)(1+\sigma^2)^{-(s+2)}\le\frac1{16}.$$ Such an $s$ is found by increasing integers and testing rational inequalities. The sequence $k(1+\sigma^2)^{-k}$ tends to zero, and is decreasing once $k>\sigma^{-2}$, since the ratio of its next term to its current term is $(1+1/k)/(1+\sigma^2)<1$. Thus these choices are effective and imply <a id="eq:hermite-tail-choice"></a>$$\label{eq:hermite-tail-choice}
 \sup_{k\ge s+2} k(1+\sigma^2)^{-k}\le\frac1{16}.$$ All constants in <a href="#eq:smoothing-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:smoothing-parameters">[eq:smoothing-parameters]</a>–<a href="#eq:hermite-cutoff-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hermite-cutoff-choice">[eq:hermite-cutoff-choice]</a> are independent of the alphabet, its proof, and the integers $m,n$.

<a id="eq:low-degree-field"></a>
The Fourier degree of a function of $B$ is its degree in the independent *rows*. More precisely, a product character is $$\chi_{\boldsymbol\alpha}(B)
 =\prod_{(i,y)\in\mathcal R}(-1)^{\alpha^i_y(B^i_y)},
 \qquad \alpha^i_y\in D(A)^*,$$ and its degree is the number of nonzero $\alpha^i_y$. This counts nonzero rows, regardless of the dimension of each row’s vector space. Gaussian degree means total degree $|I|$ in the product Hermite basis $\psi_I(c)$. Conditional on each $\xi$, let $\Pi_B$ project onto Fourier row degree at most $Rm$, and let $\Pi_c$ project onto Hermite degree at most $s$. They act on different variables and commute. Define $$\label{eq:low-degree-field}
 L=\Pi_B\Pi_c h.$$ The definition is coordinatewise in $x$. Both projections also act orthogonally in the Hilbert space averaged over the arbitrary weights of $\xi$.

<a id="stmt-lem-smoothing-cutoffs"></a>
<div id="lem:smoothing-cutoffs" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:gradient-lower"></a>
<a id="eq:c-tail"></a>
<a id="eq:B-tail"></a>
<a id="eq:L-energy"></a>
**Lemma 8** (Gradient energy and the two cutoffs). *Assume $\varepsilon_1\le Jb_1$ and $\varepsilon_2\le Jb_2$, and use <a href="#eq:smoothing-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:smoothing-parameters">[eq:smoothing-parameters]</a>–<a href="#eq:hermite-cutoff-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hermite-cutoff-choice">[eq:hermite-cutoff-choice]</a>. For every $m,n\ge2$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E\lVert h\rVert_{2,x}^2&\ge\frac34,\label{eq:gradient-lower}\\
 \mathbb E\lVert(I-\Pi_c)h\rVert_{2,x}^2&\le\frac1{16},\label{eq:c-tail}\\
 \mathbb E\lVert(I-\Pi_B)h\rVert_{2,x}^2&\le\frac1{32},\label{eq:B-tail}\\
 \mathbb E\langle L,h\rangle_x=\mathbb E\lVert L\rVert_{2,x}^2&\ge\frac12,
 \qquad \mathbb E\lVert L\rVert_{2,x}^2\le\sigma^{-2}.
 \label{eq:L-energy}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-smoothing-cutoffs"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-smoothing-cutoffs</summary>

<a id="eq:h-convolution"></a>
*Proof.* Set $Q_B(u)=\mathbb E_\lambda f(P_{u,\eta\lambda}(B))$. This is a bounded measurable function of $u$: it is obtained from finitely many threshold signs followed by a function on a finite set. Gaussian convolution and <a href="#eq:gaussian-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gaussian-derivative">[eq:gaussian-derivative]</a> give the identity $$\label{eq:h-convolution}
 h_x(B,c)=\frac{\sqrt N}{\sigma}
 \mathbb E_{g,\lambda}g_xf(P_{c+\sigma g,\eta\lambda}(B)).$$ For fixed $\xi,B,c$, the functions $g_x$ form an orthonormal family in the joint probability space of $(g,\lambda)$. Bessel’s inequality gives $$\lVert h\rVert_{2,x}^2
 =\frac1{\sigma^2}\sum_x
 \left|\mathbb E_{g,\lambda}g_xf(P(B))\right|^2
 \le\frac1{\sigma^2}\mathbb E_{g,\lambda}|f(P(B))|^2
 =\sigma^{-2}.$$ The individual-coordinate bound in <a href="#eq:gradient-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gradient-bounds">[eq:gradient-bounds]</a> follows as well. In particular all gradients used here are square-integrable.

Negating $u$ and changing $\lambda$ to $-\lambda$ negates every table entry almost surely. Indeed, conditional on $u$, each entry has an independent continuous perturbation of variance $\eta^2>0$. Folding therefore implies $Q_B(-u)=-Q_B(u)$. Symmetry of $g$ gives $F_B(-c)=-F_B(c)$, so $\mathbb E_c F_B(c)=0$ for every $\xi,B$. Let $f_0(B,c)=f(P_{c,0}(B))$, which has absolute value one. Jensen’s inequality and the first test give $$\mathbb E|F_B(c)-f_0(B,c)|^2
 \le\mathbb E_{\xi,B,c,g,\lambda}|f(P(B))-f_0(B,c)|^2
 =4\varepsilon_1\le40J\sigma\le\frac1{64}.$$ The triangle inequality in the joint $L^2$ space consequently gives $\lVert F\rVert_2\ge1-1/8=7/8$ and $\mathbb EF^2\ge49/64>3/4$. By the mean-zero Gaussian energy identity <a href="#eq:hermite-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hermite-energy">[eq:hermite-energy]</a>, $$\mathbb E\lVert h\rVert_{2,x}^2=\mathbb E\sum_x|\partial_{c_x}F_B(c)|^2
 \ge\mathbb E|F_B(c)|^2\ge\frac34,$$ proving <a href="#eq:gradient-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gradient-lower">[eq:gradient-lower]</a>. This argument only uses the averaged failure bound.

For the Gaussian cutoff put $$\rho=(1+\sigma^2)^{-1/2},\qquad
 G_B(v)=Q_B(\sqrt{1+\sigma^2}\,v).$$ Then $F_B=T_\rho G_B$ and $|G_B|\le1$. If $G_B=\sum_I a_I(B)\psi_I$, its noise average has coefficients $\rho^{|I|}a_I(B)$. For precision about the derivative, Gaussian integration by parts and the Hermite recurrence give $$\int\partial_{c_x}F_B(c)\psi_J(c)\,\mathrm d\gamma_N(c)
 =\sqrt{J_x+1}\int F_B(c)\psi_{J+e_x}(c)\,\mathrm d\gamma_N(c).$$ Integration by parts is legitimate because $F_B$ and its first derivatives are bounded, and a Gaussian times any polynomial has integrable tails. This identifies the actual derivative coefficients with those obtained by formally differentiating the Hermite expansion. Parseval and $\sum_I|a_I(B)|^2\le1$ now yield, for every $\xi,B$, $$\mathbb E_c\lVert(I-\Pi_c)h\rVert_{2,x}^2
 =\sum_{|I|\ge s+2}|I|\rho^{2|I|}|a_I(B)|^2
 \le\sup_{k\ge s+2} k(1+\sigma^2)^{-k}\le\frac1{16}.$$ The shift from $s+1$ to $s+2$ occurs because differentiating a degree $k$ Hermite polynomial gives degree $k-1$. This proves <a href="#eq:c-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:c-tail">[eq:c-tail]</a> uniformly in $N$.

<a id="eq:gradient-row-comparison"></a>
For the row cutoff, apply Bessel’s inequality to the difference of the two representations <a href="#eq:h-convolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:h-convolution">[eq:h-convolution]</a>, with the same $g,\lambda$: $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E\lVert h(B,c)-h(B',c)\rVert_{2,x}^2
 &\le\sigma^{-2}\mathbb E|f(P(B))-f(P(B'))|^2\notag\\
 &=4\varepsilon_2/\sigma^2\le4Jb_2/\sigma^2=\frac1{32}.
 \label{eq:gradient-row-comparison}
\end{aligned}$$ Write $a=\delta^2/m$. Conditional expectation over the row-resampling channel multiplies a constant character on one row by $1$, and a nonconstant character by $1-a$: on resampling, its uniform average is zero. Independence of rows therefore gives eigenvalue $(1-a)^k$ for row degree $k$. Stationarity of the channel and Parseval show that a degree-$k$ squared Fourier norm contributes $$2\bigl(1-(1-a)^k\bigr)$$ times that norm to the left side of <a href="#eq:gradient-row-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gradient-row-comparison">[eq:gradient-row-comparison]</a>. If $k>Rm$, then $ka>R\delta^2\ge1$. The elementary inequality $(1-a)^k\le(1+ka)^{-1}$ follows by applying the binomial inequality to $(1-a)^{-k}$, using $(1-a)^{-1}\ge1+a$. Hence $(1-a)^k<1/2$, and the displayed multiplier exceeds one. The total squared Fourier norm of row degrees $k>Rm$ is at most $1/32$, proving <a href="#eq:B-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:B-tail">[eq:B-tail]</a>.

Finally, commuting orthogonal projections give $\mathbb E\langle L,h\rangle_x=\mathbb E\lVert L\rVert_{2,x}^2$. Their discarded parts satisfy $$\mathbb E\lVert h-L\rVert_{2,x}^2
 \le\mathbb E\lVert(I-\Pi_B)h\rVert_{2,x}^2
     +\mathbb E\lVert(I-\Pi_c)h\rVert_{2,x}^2
 \le\frac1{32}+\frac1{16}.$$ Combining this with <a href="#eq:gradient-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gradient-lower">[eq:gradient-lower]</a> gives $\mathbb E\lVert L\rVert_{2,x}^2\ge21/32>1/2$.
