Orthogonal projection and <a href="#eq:gradient-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gradient-bounds">[eq:gradient-bounds]</a> give the upper bound in <a href="#eq:L-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L-energy">[eq:L-energy]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Truth-table differentiation and a product-of-faces correlation

For a real array $v$ on $X$, define <a id="eq:face-correlation"></a>$$\label{eq:face-correlation}
 \mathfrak d_X(v)
 =\max_{z\in\mathcal C}\left|\frac1N\sum_{x\in X}v_x(-1)^{z_x}\right|
 =\max_{z^1,\ldots,z^m}
 \left|\mathbb E_{x\in X}v_x\prod_{i=1}^m(-1)^{z^i_{x_{-i}}}\right|.$$ The second maximum is over the binary face arrays appearing in <a href="#eq:face-code" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:face-code">[eq:face-code]</a>; equality of the maxima follows from that definition. In particular $\mathfrak d_X(v)\le\lVert v\rVert_{2,x}$.

<a id="stmt-prop-correlation"></a>
<div id="prop:correlation" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:correlation-lower"></a>
**Proposition 9** (Low-degree correlation). *Fix $J$ and the constants <a href="#eq:smoothing-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:smoothing-parameters">[eq:smoothing-parameters]</a>–<a href="#eq:hermite-cutoff-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hermite-cutoff-choice">[eq:hermite-cutoff-choice]</a>. For every $m,n\ge2$ and every finite weighted family of folded proofs satisfying $\varepsilon_j\le Jb_j$ for $j=1,2,3$, the field $L$ from <a href="#eq:low-degree-field" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:low-degree-field">[eq:low-degree-field]</a> has Fourier row degree at most $Rm$ and Gaussian degree at most $s$, satisfies <a href="#eq:L-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L-energy">[eq:L-energy]</a>, and obeys $$\label{eq:correlation-lower}
 \mathbb E\mathfrak d_X(L)\ge\frac1{4T}.$$ All constants preceding $m,n$ are uniform in their values and in the affine alphabets.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-correlation"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-correlation</summary>

<a id="eq:truth-table-representation"></a>
*Proof.* It remains to prove <a href="#eq:correlation-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:correlation-lower">[eq:correlation-lower]</a>. For fixed $B$ define the bounded function on $\mathbb R^{\mathcal C}$ $$\Phi_B(t)=f\bigl(a\mapsto\operatorname{sgn}(t_{B(a)})\bigr),\qquad
 \Psi_B(t)=\mathbb E_\lambda\Phi_B(t+\eta\lambda).$$ Gaussian differentiation in the independent truth-table coordinates gives $$\partial_{t_z}\Psi_B(t)
 =\eta^{-1}\mathbb E_\lambda\lambda_z\Phi_B(t+\eta\lambda).$$ In <a href="#eq:smoothed-gradient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:smoothed-gradient">[eq:smoothed-gradient]</a>, the input to this function is $t_z=N^{-1/2}\sum_x(c_x+\sigma g_x)(-1)^{z_x}$. The chain rule therefore gives the exact representation $$\label{eq:truth-table-representation}
 h_x(B,c)=\sum_{z\in\mathcal C}\mu_z(B,c)(-1)^{z_x},\qquad
 \mu_z(B,c)=\eta^{-1}\mathbb E_{g,\lambda}\lambda_zf(P(B)).$$ There are finitely many table coordinates, and $|\partial_{t_z}\Psi_B|\le\eta^{-1}\mathbb E|\lambda_z|$ uniformly in $t$, so differentiation may pass through the expectation over $g$.

We bound the absolute coefficients by the third bit test. Condition on $\xi,B,c,g$ and on all coordinates of $\lambda$ other than $\lambda_z$. The two numbers $$f_+=f(P^{z,+}(B)),\qquad f_-=f(P^{z,-}(B))$$ do not depend on $\lambda_z$. If $t_z=N^{-1/2}\sum_x(c_x+\sigma g_x)(-1)^{z_x}$ and $\varphi(u)=(2\pi)^{-1/2}e^{-u^2/2}$, elementary integration of the one-dimensional Gaussian density yields $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E_{\lambda_z}\lambda_zf(P(B))
 &=f_+\int_{-t_z/\eta}^{\infty}u\varphi(u)\,\mathrm du
   +f_-\int_{-\infty}^{-t_z/\eta}u\varphi(u)\,\mathrm du\\
 &=(f_+-f_-)\varphi(t_z/\eta).
\end{aligned}$$ Its absolute value is at most $2\mathbf1_{\{f_+\ne f_-\}}$. This calculation depends only on the two possible table values at $z$; it does not require $B:A\to\mathcal C$ to be injective. Taking absolute values after the other averages and summing over $z$ gives, with $W=\sum_{z\in\mathcal C}|\mu_z|$, <a id="eq:truth-table-mass"></a>$$\label{eq:truth-table-mass}
 \mathbb EW\le\frac2\eta\sum_{z\in\mathcal C}
       \mathbb P[f(P^{z,+}(B))\ne f(P^{z,-}(B))]
 =\frac{2|\mathcal C|}\eta\varepsilon_3\le\frac{2J}\eta.$$ Thus <a href="#eq:truth-table-representation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:truth-table-representation">[eq:truth-table-representation]</a> places $h$ in $W$ times the convex hull of the signed codeword arrays $\{\pm((-1)^{z_x})_{x\in X}:z\in\mathcal C\}$ (with $h=0$ if $W=0$). For every realization it follows that <a id="eq:convex-hull-correlation"></a>$$\label{eq:convex-hull-correlation}
 |\langle L,h\rangle_x|\le W\mathfrak d_X(L).$$

The large-$W$ contribution can be controlled without a pointwise bound on $L$. By <a href="#eq:gradient-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gradient-bounds">[eq:gradient-bounds]</a>, Cauchy–Schwarz, Markov’s inequality, and <a href="#eq:L-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:L-energy">[eq:L-energy]</a>, $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E\bigl[\mathbf1_{\{W>T\}}|\langle L,h\rangle_x|\bigr]
 &\le\sigma^{-1}\mathbb E\bigl[\mathbf1_{\{W>T\}}\lVert L\rVert_{2,x}\bigr]\\
 &\le\sigma^{-1}(\mathbb E\lVert L\rVert_{2,x}^2)^{1/2}
                 \mathbb P[W>T]^{1/2}\\
 &\le\sigma^{-2}\sqrt{\frac{2J}{\eta T}}\le\frac14.
\end{aligned}$$ Since $\mathbb E\langle L,h\rangle_x\ge1/2$, we obtain $$\frac14
 \le\mathbb E\bigl[\mathbf1_{\{W\le T\}}\langle L,h\rangle_x\bigr]
 \le T\mathbb E\mathfrak d_X(L),$$ which is <a href="#eq:correlation-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:correlation-lower">[eq:correlation-lower]</a>. The low-degree and energy claims have already been proved in Lemma <a href="#lem:smoothing-cutoffs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:smoothing-cutoffs">8</a>. ◻

</details>

At this stage $m,n$ remain free. The next Section chooses $m$ from $R,s$, then a localization scale and $n$, and finally $p,\theta$. It uses <a href="#eq:correlation-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:correlation-lower">[eq:correlation-lower]</a> to prove the asserted one-row Fourier atom. In particular, the possibly large truth-table truncation constant $T$ has been chosen before any of these later parameters.

<a id="sec:inner-box"></a>
### From face correlation to an odd row coefficient

Proposition <a href="#prop:correlation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:correlation">9</a> gives a low-degree field $L$ with $\mathbb E\mathfrak d_X(L)\ge1/(4T)$. We will show that this correlation forces a large odd Fourier coefficient in one row of the original field $h$, as required by Theorem <a href="#thm:inner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:inner">7</a>. We first choose $m$ large enough to partition the Fourier–Hermite terms of $L_x$: each nonzero term either has positive degree in $c_x$, or carries equal odd frequencies in two rows $B^i_{x_{-i}}$ and $B^j_{x_{-j}}$. Equal frequencies turn the two row factors into a character of their sum. Restriction to a random smaller box will then separate the other variables, allowing the two kinds of terms to be estimated by centering and by a four-corner Fourier identity.

Throughout this Section, $\sigma,\delta,\eta,R,s,T$ are the constants of Proposition <a href="#prop:correlation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:correlation">9</a>, already chosen independently of $m,n$ and of the affine alphabet. All expectations include the weighted proof index $\xi$, unless explicitly conditioned on it. We use only averaged test bounds; individual indices need not satisfy those bounds.

Let $$\mathcal R=\{(i,y):i\in[m],\ y\in[n]^{[m]\setminus\{i\}}\},
 \qquad M=|\mathcal R|=mn^{m-1}$$ be the set of rows. For a family of frequencies $\boldsymbol\alpha=(\alpha_r:r\in\mathcal R)$, write $\chi_{\boldsymbol\alpha}(B)=\prod_r\chi_{\alpha_r}(B_r)$ and $S_i=\{y:\alpha^i_y\ne0\}$. Thus $S=(S_i)_i$ is the exact row mask, and $|S|=\sum_i|S_i|$ is the row degree. At a fixed proof index, the Fourier–Hermite expansion of the projected field is <a id="eq:box-expansion"></a>$$\label{eq:box-expansion}
 L_x(B,c)=
 \sum_{\substack{|I|\le s\\|S(\boldsymbol\alpha)|\le Rm}}
 \widehat h_x(\boldsymbol\alpha,I)
 \chi_{\boldsymbol\alpha}(B)\psi_I(c).$$ This is a finite sum. Coefficients and the row frequency spaces may depend on $\xi$; the sets of row positions and Hermite indices do not. We select components using only $x,I,S$. Counting row masks and Hermite indices, without counting the possible values of the nonzero frequencies, will keep the coefficient estimates independent of the alphabet.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Gauge identities and a partition selected by masks

<a id="eq:cylinder-gauge"></a>
We use two symmetries of $h$ to find these equal, odd local frequencies. First fix distinct axes $i,j$ and a tuple $q\in[n]^{[m]\setminus\{i,j\}}$. For $a\in D(A)$, add $a$ to every row $B^i_y$ with $y_{-j}=q$ and every row $B^j_y$ with $y_{-i}=q$. Call the resulting array $B^{(a)}$. At a point $u\in X$, either both additions occur or neither occurs, so $B^{(a)}(u)=B(u)$. Consequently $h_x(B^{(a)},c)=h_x(B,c)$. Orthogonality, or a change of variables in the Fourier coefficient, shows that every nonzero coefficient satisfies $$\label{eq:cylinder-gauge}
 \sum_{y:y_{-j}=q}\alpha^i_y
 +\sum_{y:y_{-i}=q}\alpha^j_y=0
 \qquad\text{in }D(A)^*.$$ Indeed, otherwise some $a$ makes the character change sign while the function remains unchanged.

<a id="eq:row-gauge"></a>
For the second symmetry fix a row $r=(i,v)$ and put $k_u=\mathbf 1_{\{u_{-i}=v\}}$. This is a codeword in $\mathcal C$. Let $B+1_r$ denote addition of the constant-one affine function in this row, and let $D_kc=((-1)^{k_u}c_u)_{u\in X}$. Translation of a codeword argument by $k$ gives the exact identity $$P_{c+\sigma g,\eta\lambda}(z+k)
 =P_{D_kc+\sigma D_kg,\eta\lambda'}(z),
 \qquad \lambda'_z=\lambda_{z+k}.$$ The Gaussian vectors $D_kg$ and $\lambda'$ have the original joint law. The proof is a function of the resulting Boolean table on $A$, so $$F_{B+1_r}(c)=F_B(D_kc),\qquad
 h_x(B+1_r,c)=(-1)^{k_x}h_x(B,D_kc).$$ The derivative in the second equality supplies its displayed sign. Since $\psi_I(D_kc)=(-1)^{\sum_u k_u I_u}\psi_I(c)$, every nonzero coefficient also satisfies $$\label{eq:row-gauge}
 \alpha_r(1)\equiv k_x+\sum_{u\in X}k_uI_u\pmod2.$$ These identities hold separately for every proof index and for every alphabet.

<a id="eq:choose-m"></a>
Choose $m>s+1$ so large that $$\label{eq:choose-m}
 \binom{m-s}{2}>Rm.$$ Partition the terms of $L_x$ as follows. Those with $I_x>0$ form class $0$. For each remaining pair $(I,S)$, choose two axes $i<j$ subject to:

1.  No $u$ with $I_u>0$ satisfies $u_{-i}=x_{-i}$ or $u_{-j}=x_{-j}$.

2.  Among rows of $S_i$ above $x_{-ij}$, the only permitted row is $x_{-i}$; among rows of $S_j$ above $x_{-ij}$, the only permitted row is $x_{-j}$.

Such a pair exists. Since $I_x=0$, each positive Hermite support point differs from $x$. It can agree with $x$ after deleting an axis for at most one axis, so at most $s$ axes are forbidden by (i). A nonlocal row $y\in S_i$, $y\ne x_{-i}$, obstructs (ii) for at most one other axis $j$: its coordinates must differ from $x_{-i}$ only at $j$. Thus all masks together forbid at most $Rm$ unordered pairs. Inequality <a href="#eq:choose-m" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:choose-m">[eq:choose-m]</a> leaves an admissible pair.

<a id="eq:matching-odd"></a>
Fix an ordering of the pairs and choose the first admissible one. This rule uses only $x,I,S$, never the values of the nonzero frequencies. Assign the entire exact-mask/Hermite component to that pair class. For a nonzero term in class $(i,j)$, apply <a href="#eq:cylinder-gauge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cylinder-gauge">[eq:cylinder-gauge]</a> with $q=x_{-ij}$. Condition (ii) leaves only the two local frequencies, so they are equal. Apply <a href="#eq:row-gauge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:row-gauge">[eq:row-gauge]</a> to $r=(i,x_{-i})$. Here $k_x=1$ and condition (i) makes the Hermite sum zero. Therefore $$\label{eq:matching-odd}
 \alpha^i_{x_{-i}}=\alpha^j_{x_{-j}},
 \qquad \alpha^i_{x_{-i}}(1)=1.$$ In particular both local rows really occur in the mask. Equality and oddness are consequences of the two symmetries, rather than extra conditions used to filter individual frequencies.

<a id="eq:class-target"></a>
<a id="eq:class-correlation-lower"></a>
Write $L^a$ for the class arrays, with $a\in\mathcal K=\{0\}\cup\{(i,j):1\le i<j\le m\}$, and set $$\label{eq:class-target}
 d_0=\frac{1}{4T(1+\binom m2)}.$$ By subadditivity of $\mathfrak d_X$ and Proposition <a href="#prop:correlation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:correlation">9</a>, $$\label{eq:class-correlation-lower}
 \frac1{4T}\le\mathbb E\mathfrak d_X(L)
 \le\sum_{a\in\mathcal K}\mathbb E\mathfrak d_X(L^a).$$ It will suffice to show, under failure of the asserted row-atom conclusion, that every summand is strictly less than $d_0$.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### Localization to a random subbox

<a id="eq:box-restriction"></a>
Let $2\le\ell<n$, with both integers to be chosen later. Independently on each axis choose a uniform $\ell$-element subset $Y_i\subset[n]$, and put $Y=\prod_iY_i$. The average of a uniform point in $Y$, over this random choice, is uniform in $X$. For every deterministic array $v$, $$\label{eq:box-restriction}
 \mathfrak d_X(v)\le\mathbb E_Y\mathfrak d_Y(v|_Y).$$ To prove this, choose face signs attaining $\mathfrak d_X(v)$, restrict them to $Y$, and use that the original correlation is the average of their restricted correlations. The absolute value of the average is at most the average of the absolute values. This is a pointwise assertion and allows the maximizing face signs to depend on the full array.

For $x\in Y$, retain from $L^a_x$ only those terms whose positive Hermite support on $Y$ is contained in $\{x\}$ and whose masked rows on the faces of $Y$ are contained in $\{(r,x_{-r}):r\in[m]\}$. Denote the retained field by $v^a_x$. Equivalently, $v^a_x=Q^{a,Y}_xh_x$, where $Q^{a,Y}_x$ is an orthogonal projection selecting complete exact-mask/Hermite components. In particular its choice depends only on $a,Y,x,I,S$.

<a id="eq:retained-energy"></a>
Conditional on a uniform point of $Y$ being $x$, the subsets $Y_i$ are independent uniform $\ell$-subsets containing $x_i$. For a fixed point $u\ne x$, membership in $Y$ requires that at least one particular value other than $x_i$ be selected, so its conditional probability is at most $(\ell-1)/(n-1)$. The same assertion holds for a face tuple different from the corresponding face of $x$. A term has at most $s$ positive Hermite support positions and $Rm$ masked rows. A union bound, followed by orthogonality for fixed $x,Y$, therefore gives <a id="eq:localization-l2"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E\mathbb E_{x\in Y}|L^a_x-v^a_x|^2
 &\le \sigma^{-2}(s+Rm)\frac{\ell}{n-1},
 \label{eq:localization-l2}\\
 \mathbb E\mathbb E_{x\in Y}|v^a_x|^2&\le\sigma^{-2}.
 \label{eq:retained-energy}
\end{aligned}$$ For the first inequality one can sum the squared coefficients of $L^a_x$ multiplied by their conditional removal probabilities; their total, averaged over uniform $x$ and $\xi$, is at most $\sigma^{-2}$. For the second, each retained projection contracts the squared norm of $h_x$, and then one averages over $x,Y,\xi$. Thus the expected normalized $L^1$ loss in localization is at most <a id="eq:localization-loss"></a>$$\label{eq:localization-loss}
 e_{\mathrm{loc}}=
 \sigma^{-1}\sqrt{(s+Rm)\frac{\ell}{n-1}}.$$ No pointwise bound on a retained field is needed here.

There are two useful conditional descriptions. For class $0$, condition on $\xi,Y$, all rows $B$, and all Gaussians outside $Y$. Each $v^0_x$ is then a function of $c_x$ alone, and its conditional mean is zero: every retained Hermite term has $I_x>0$ and no other positive index on $Y$.

<a id="eq:local-sum-slice"></a>
<a id="eq:slice-energy"></a>
For class $(i,j)$, condition on $\xi,Y$, all Gaussians, all tables other than $B^i,B^j$, and the rows of those two tables outside the faces of $Y$. Call this background $\mathcal F_{ij,Y}$. The remaining rows of these two tables are still independent and uniform. Localization and <a href="#eq:matching-odd" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matching-odd">[eq:matching-odd]</a> give, for each $x\in Y$, a function $V_x:D(A)\to\mathbb R$, determined by this background, such that $$\label{eq:local-sum-slice}
 v^{ij}_x=V_x(B^i_{x_{-i}}+B^j_{x_{-j}}),
 \qquad
 V_x(b)=\sum_{\alpha:\alpha(1)=1}\widehat V_x(\alpha)\chi_\alpha(b).$$ Indeed, a retained term has no other on-subbox row in either table, and its two local character factors multiply to the character of their sum. Thus $V_x$ is independent of the sampled values of every on-subbox row in tables $i,j$. The slice norm $\|V_x\|_2$ and its maximum Fourier coefficient are functions of $\mathcal F_{ij,Y}$ as well. Integrating the two local rows makes their sum uniform, and hence <a href="#eq:retained-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:retained-energy">[eq:retained-energy]</a> says $$\label{eq:slice-energy}
 \mathbb E\mathbb E_{x\in Y}\|V_x\|_2^2\le\sigma^{-2}.$$

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### A coefficient estimate with no dependence on the alphabet

The next estimate connects the projected slices to the original field. For an axis $i$ and point $x$, put $r=(i,x_{-i})$ and define the original maximum odd row coefficient by $$a_{x,i}(B_{\ne r},c)=
 \max_{\alpha:\alpha(1)=1}
 \left|\mathbb E_{b\in D(A)}h_x(B[r\leftarrow b],c)\chi_\alpha(b)\right|.$$ This is a function only of the indicated background and the proof index. Its pointwise upper bound is $\sqrt N/\sigma$.

<a id="stmt-lem-projected-row-atom"></a>
<div id="lem:projected-row-atom" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:projector-maximum"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Uniform transfer through the selected projection). *Let $Q_x^Y$ select any collection of complete exact-mask/Hermite components with $|I|\le s$, using only $x,Y,I,S$. For a fixed axis $i$ let $q_{x,i}^Y$ be the maximum odd row coefficient of $Q_x^Yh_x$ in $r=(i,x_{-i})$, with the other rows and $c$ held fixed. Then $$\label{eq:projector-maximum}
 \mathbb E\mathbb E_{x\in Y}q_{x,i}^Y
 \le C_0\left(\mathbb E\mathbb E_{x\in X}a_{x,i}^2\right)^{1/2},
 \qquad C_0=4^M\binom{N+s}{s}.$$ The estimate is valid for every weighted distribution of proof indices.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-projected-row-atom"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-projected-row-atom</summary>

*Proof.* For a row $t$, write $E_t$ for averaging that row uniformly. The projection onto an exact mask $S\subseteq\mathcal R$ is $$P_S=\prod_{t\in S}(\operatorname{Id}-E_t)
       \prod_{t\notin S}E_t.$$ The projection onto the Hermite index $I$ is $$H_Iu(B,c)=\psi_I(c)\mathbb E_{c'}\psi_I(c')u(B,c').$$ There are $2^M$ masks and $\binom{N+s}{s}$ multi-indices with $|I|\le s$. Thus $Q_x^Y$ is a sum of at most their product of operators $P_SH_I$.

Taking a Fourier coefficient in the distinguished row $r$ commutes with all the other row averages. If $r\notin S$, the average $E_r$ annihilates every odd coefficient. If $r\in S$, the factor $\operatorname{Id}-E_r$ leaves such a coefficient unchanged. Expand the remaining factors $\operatorname{Id}-E_t$ into signed averages; there are at most $2^M$ terms. Each term averages some of the other rows without changing the distinguished frequency. For one such term, its maximum odd coefficient is consequently bounded by $$|\psi_I(c)|\,
 \mathbb E_{c',\,\text{averaged rows}}
       |\psi_I(c')|\,a_{x,i}(B'_{\ne r},c').$$ This follows by moving the maximum inside the absolute-value integral; no sum over row frequencies is introduced. At fixed $\xi,x$, average the output $c$ and all the other output rows. Uniform row averages preserve the product measure, $\mathbb E|\psi_I(c)|\le1$, and Cauchy–Schwarz gives $$\mathbb E_{B_{\ne r},c'}|\psi_I(c')|a_{x,i}(B_{\ne r},c')
 \le\left(\mathbb E_{B_{\ne r},c'}a_{x,i}^2\right)^{1/2}.$$ The bound is independent of $I,S,Y$. Summing at most $4^M\binom{N+s}{s}$ expanded terms proves the corresponding bound at fixed $\xi,x,Y$. Finally apply Cauchy–Schwarz to the probability measure on $\xi,Y,x\in Y$, and use the uniform marginal of $x$. This proves <a href="#eq:projector-maximum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projector-maximum">[eq:projector-maximum]</a>, including arbitrary weights on $\xi$. Only counts of positions, masks and Hermite indices entered the constant. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:original-atom-square"></a>
Suppose, for a contradiction, that the row-atom conclusion in Theorem <a href="#thm:inner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:inner">7</a> fails for positive constants $p,\theta$ to be chosen at the end. In this notation the failed assertion is $$\mathbb P_{\xi,x,i,B_{\ne(i,x_{-i})},c}[a_{x,i}\ge\theta]<p.$$ Since $i$ is uniform, for each fixed axis $i$ this event has probability less than $mp$. The pointwise bound on $a_{x,i}$ gives $$\label{eq:original-atom-square}
 \mathbb E\mathbb E_{x\in X}a_{x,i}^2\le D,
 \qquad D=\theta^2+\frac{mpN}{\sigma^2}.$$ This is only an averaged estimate in $x,\xi$, which is exactly the estimate required in Lemma <a href="#lem:projected-row-atom" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:projected-row-atom">10</a>.

<a id="eq:slice-atom-probability"></a>
For a retained pair class use $Q_x^Y=Q_x^{ij,Y}$ and the distinguished axis $i$. From <a href="#eq:local-sum-slice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-sum-slice">[eq:local-sum-slice]</a>, its coefficient in the row $B^i_{x_{-i}}$, when the other local row has value $b'$, is $\widehat V_x(\alpha)\chi_\alpha(b')$. The maximum magnitude is therefore precisely $q_x=\max_{\alpha:\alpha(1)=1}|\widehat V_x(\alpha)|$, independently of $b'$ or any other remaining row. Equations <a href="#eq:projector-maximum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projector-maximum">[eq:projector-maximum]</a>–<a href="#eq:original-atom-square" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:original-atom-square">[eq:original-atom-square]</a> imply $$\label{eq:slice-atom-probability}
 \mathbb E\mathbb E_{x\in Y}q_x\le C_0\sqrt D,
 \qquad
 \mathbb P[q_x>\gamma]\le\frac{C_0\sqrt D}{\gamma}$$ for every $\gamma>0$. The probability here is over a uniform point of $Y$ and its slice background.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### Bounded arrays and background-measurable slice operations

<a id="eq:clip-elementary"></a>
For $A_0>0$, let $\operatorname{clip}_{A_0}(z)=
\max(-A_0,\min(z,A_0))$. We use $$\label{eq:clip-elementary}
 |z-\operatorname{clip}_{A_0}(z)|\le z^2/A_0.$$

For class $0$, use its conditional description above and set $$w^0_x=\operatorname{clip}_{A_0}(v^0_x)
       -\mathbb E_{c_x}\operatorname{clip}_{A_0}(v^0_x).$$ Here the conditional expectation fixes all rows and the Gaussians outside $Y$. Since $\mathbb E_{c_x}v^0_x=0$, inequality <a href="#eq:clip-elementary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:clip-elementary">[eq:clip-elementary]</a> bounds both the clipping loss and the absolute value of the subtracted mean. Thus <a id="eq:class-zero-processing"></a>$$\label{eq:class-zero-processing}
 |w^0_x|\le2A_0,\qquad
 \mathbb E\mathbb E_{x\in Y}|v^0_x-w^0_x|\le\frac{2\sigma^{-2}}{A_0}.$$ Conditioned on that common background, the variables $w^0_x$, $x\in Y$, depend on distinct independent Gaussians and have mean zero.

For class $(i,j)$, operate on the entire slice $V_x$ as follows:

1.  Set the slice to zero if $\|V_x\|_2>H$.

2.  Set it to zero if $q_x>\gamma$.

3.  Clip every value of a remaining slice to $[-A_0,A_0]$.

Both discard decisions depend only on $\mathcal F_{ij,Y}$. In particular, a decision is made from the function of the free sum variable and is independent of the sampled local rows. Let $W_x$ be the resulting slice and $w^{ij}_x=W_x(B^i_{x_{-i}}+B^j_{x_{-j}})$.

Write $\nu_x=\|V_x\|_2$. The first discard costs at most $$\mathbb E\mathbb E_{x\in Y}\nu_x\mathbf 1_{\{\nu_x>H\}}
 \le H^{-1}\mathbb E\mathbb E_{x\in Y}\nu_x^2\le\frac{\sigma^{-2}}H.$$ For the second discard, Cauchy–Schwarz, <a href="#eq:slice-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:slice-energy">[eq:slice-energy]</a> and <a href="#eq:slice-atom-probability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:slice-atom-probability">[eq:slice-atom-probability]</a> give the bound <a id="eq:atom-discard-loss"></a>$$\label{eq:atom-discard-loss}
 e_{\mathrm{atom}}=
 \sigma^{-1}\sqrt{C_0/\gamma}\,D^{1/4}.$$ Indeed its loss is at most $\mathbb E\mathbb E_{x\in Y}\nu_x\mathbf 1_{\{q_x>\gamma\}}$, regardless of whether the first discard has already occurred. The last clipping operation costs at most $\sigma^{-2}/A_0$ by <a href="#eq:clip-elementary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:clip-elementary">[eq:clip-elementary]</a>. Consequently <a id="eq:pair-processing-loss"></a>$$\label{eq:pair-processing-loss}
 \mathbb E\mathbb E_{x\in Y}|v^{ij}_x-w^{ij}_x|
 \le\frac{\sigma^{-2}}H+e_{\mathrm{atom}}+
       \frac{\sigma^{-2}}{A_0}.$$

Every processed slice has absolute bound $A_0$ and $L^2$ norm at most $H$. For a slice surviving both discards, its clipping error in conditional $L^1(D(A))$ is at most $H^2/A_0$. Each Fourier coefficient changes by at most this amount. Before clipping the spectrum was odd and all its coefficients had magnitude at most $\gamma$, so, for all frequencies, <a id="eq:processed-slice-bounds"></a>$$\label{eq:processed-slice-bounds}
 \|W_x\|_2\le H,\qquad
 \max_\alpha|\widehat W_x(\alpha)|\le\varepsilon_0,
 \qquad \varepsilon_0=\gamma+H^2/A_0.$$ Discarded slices satisfy the same bounds. Symmetric clipping in fact preserves oddness, since $V_x(b+1)=-V_x(b)$, although the following estimate only needs the bound for all coefficients.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Box Cauchy–Schwarz and the independent squares

We prove the deterministic inequality used to remove the face signs. This is the iterated Cauchy–Schwarz mechanism of the box norms in hypergraph-uniformity arguments (Gowers 2007, sec. 3.5 and Lemma 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Gowers2007](#ref-Gowers2007)]. The conditional square calculation that follows is specific to our localized array. For a finite product $Y=\prod_{j=1}^mY_j$ and an array $v:Y\to\mathbb R$, let $u^0,u^1$ be independent uniform points of $Y$ and set $u^\omega=(u_1^{\omega_1},\ldots,u_m^{\omega_m})$ for $\omega\in\{0,1\}^m$.

<a id="stmt-lem-box-cs"></a>
<div id="lem:box-cs" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:box-cs"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Box Cauchy–Schwarz). *For every real array $v$, $$\label{eq:box-cs}
 \mathfrak d_Y(v)^{2^m}
 \le\mathbb E_{u^0,u^1}\prod_{\omega\in\{0,1\}^m}v(u^\omega).$$ In particular the right side is nonnegative.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-box-cs"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-box-cs</summary>

*Proof.* Fix face multipliers $a_j:Y_{-j}\to[-1,1]$. We describe each Cauchy–Schwarz step. At stage $k$, double the first $k$ coordinates and keep the last $m-k$ coordinates single. For $\omega\in\{0,1\}^k$, write $$z^\omega=(u_1^{\omega_1},\ldots,u_k^{\omega_k},
                 u_{k+1},\ldots,u_m)$$ and define $$A_k=\mathbb E\left[
   \prod_{\omega\in\{0,1\}^k}v(z^\omega)
   \prod_{j=k+1}^m\prod_{\omega\in\{0,1\}^k}
                 a_j(z^\omega_{-j})\right].$$ Thus $A_0$ is the original correlation and $A_m$ is the right side of <a href="#eq:box-cs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:box-cs">[eq:box-cs]</a>. In $A_{k-1}$, the product of factors $a_k$ is independent of the single coordinate $u_k$ and has absolute value at most one. Put the average over $u_k$ innermost and apply Cauchy–Schwarz over all outer variables, removing that product. The result is the average of the square of the remaining inner average. Expanding this square with two independent values $u_k^0,u_k^1$ gives exactly $A_k$. Therefore $$|A_{k-1}|^2\le A_k\qquad(1\le k\le m).$$ Iterating yields $|A_0|^{2^m}\le A_m$, as well as nonnegativity of $A_m$. Taking the maximum over the finitely many face signs proves the assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:box-outer-average"></a>
Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:box-cs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:box-cs">11</a> pointwise to a processed random field $w$, before averaging any of its randomness. Write $Q=2^m$. By Jensen’s inequality, $$\label{eq:box-outer-average}
 (\mathbb E\mathfrak d_Y(w))^Q
 \le\mathbb E\mathfrak d_Y(w)^Q
 \le\mathbb E\mathbb E_{u^0,u^1}\prod_\omega w(u^\omega).$$ The choices with $u_i^0=u_i^1$ for at least one $i$ have probability at most $m/\ell$. Since all processed fields have bound $2A_0$, their contribution to the last expectation is at most $(m/\ell)(2A_0)^Q$ in absolute value.

On a cube with no repeated coordinates, all its vertices are distinct. For class $0$, condition on all rows and on the Gaussians outside $Y$. The factors $w^0(u^\omega)$ are independent centered functions of distinct $c_{u^\omega}$, so their product has conditional mean zero. Thus <a id="eq:class-zero-correlation"></a>$$\label{eq:class-zero-correlation}
 \mathbb E\mathfrak d_Y(w^0)\le
 2A_0(m/\ell)^{1/2^m}.$$

For class $(i,j)$, condition instead on $\mathcal F_{ij,Y}$ and on the chosen cube without repetitions. Partition its vertices into $2^{m-2}$ squares by fixing all coordinates other than $i,j$. On any one square its four local sums have the form $$r_a+s_b,\qquad (a,b)\in\{0,1\}^2,$$ where $r_0,r_1$ are the two rows of table $B^j$, and $s_0,s_1$ the two rows of table $B^i$. These four rows are independent and uniform in $D(A)$. Figure <a href="#fig:square" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:square">1</a> displays the shared rows. The four sums are uniform subject to their zero-sum relation; they are not four independent variables.

<figure id="fig:square">

<figcaption>A square after fixing the slice background. The four rows $r_{0}, r_{1}, s_{0}, s_{1}$ are independent, but their four sums satisfy one parity relation. Fourier expansion therefore leaves one common frequency. Different squares of a collision-free cube use disjoint rows.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-square"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:square`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[every node/.style={font=\small},scale=1.15]
\node (a) at (0,2) {$W_{00}(r_0+s_0)$};
\node (b) at (4,2) {$W_{01}(r_0+s_1)$};
\node (c) at (0,0) {$W_{10}(r_1+s_0)$};
\node (d) at (4,0) {$W_{11}(r_1+s_1)$};
\draw (a) -- node[above] {shared row $r_0$} (b);
\draw (c) -- node[below] {shared row $r_1$} (d);
\draw (a) -- node[left] {$s_0$} (c);
\draw (b) -- node[right] {$s_1$} (d);
\end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 20 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A square diagram showing four vertices connected by four straight edges:
- Top-left vertex labeled $W_{00}(r_0 + s_0)$
- Top-right vertex labeled $W_{01}(r_0 + s_1)$
- Bottom-left vertex labeled $W_{10}(r_1 + s_0)$
- Bottom-right vertex labeled $W_{11}(r_1 + s_1)$
- Top horizontal edge labeled above with "shared row $r_0$"
- Bottom horizontal edge labeled below with "shared row $r_1$"
- Left vertical edge labeled to the left with "$s_0$"
- Right vertical edge labeled to the right with "$s_1$"
```


<a id="eq:square-fourier"></a>
For four possibly different real slice functions $W_{ab}$, direct Fourier expansion gives $$\label{eq:square-fourier}
 \mathbb E_{r_0,r_1,s_0,s_1}
       \prod_{a,b\in\{0,1\}}W_{ab}(r_a+s_b)
 =\sum_\alpha\prod_{a,b\in\{0,1\}}\widehat W_{ab}(\alpha).$$ Indeed averaging $r_0,r_1$ forces the two frequencies in each row of the square to agree, and averaging $s_0,s_1$ forces agreement between these rows. Using the coefficient bound on two factors and Cauchy–Schwarz and Parseval on the other two, the absolute value of <a href="#eq:square-fourier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:square-fourier">[eq:square-fourier]</a> is at most <a id="eq:square-bound"></a>$$\label{eq:square-bound}
 \varepsilon_0^2\sum_\alpha
       |\widehat W_{10}(\alpha)\widehat W_{11}(\alpha)|
 \le\varepsilon_0^2H^2.$$

<a id="eq:pair-correlation"></a>
Different squares use disjoint rows in both tables: any change in one of the other $m-2$ coordinates changes a retained coordinate of every such row. Conditional on the fixed background, their products are consequently independent. Their slice functions may share that background, which does not affect this independence. The absolute conditional expectation of the full cube product is therefore at most $(\varepsilon_0^2H^2)^{2^{m-2}}$. Combining this with the collision bound and <a href="#eq:box-outer-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:box-outer-average">[eq:box-outer-average]</a>, and using $(a+b)^{1/Q}\le a^{1/Q}+b^{1/Q}$ for $a,b\ge0$, gives $$\label{eq:pair-correlation}
 \mathbb E\mathfrak d_Y(w^{ij})
 \le2A_0(m/\ell)^{1/2^m}
     +\sqrt{H(\gamma+H^2/A_0)}.$$ The maximizing face signs were eliminated by the deterministic inequality before the conditional independence argument was used. No independence assumption about those signs is involved.

<a id="sec:inner-completion"></a>
#### Choosing parameters and completing inner decoding

<a id="eq:six-errors"></a>
<a id="eq:zero-errors"></a>
For any two fields on a fixed box, $|\mathfrak d_Y(u)-\mathfrak d_Y(v)|\le\mathbb E_{x\in Y}|u_x-v_x|$. Thus <a href="#eq:box-restriction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:box-restriction">[eq:box-restriction]</a>, localization, the processing losses, and <a href="#eq:pair-correlation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-correlation">[eq:pair-correlation]</a> imply, for each pair class, $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E\mathfrak d_X(L^{ij})
 \le{}&\underbrace{\sigma^{-1}
       \sqrt{(s+Rm)\ell/(n-1)}}_{e_{\mathrm{loc}}}
       +\frac{\sigma^{-2}}H
       +\underbrace{\sigma^{-1}\sqrt{C_0/\gamma}
           (\theta^2+mpN/\sigma^2)^{1/4}}_{e_{\mathrm{atom}}}
       \notag\\
      &+\frac{\sigma^{-2}}{A_0}
       +2A_0(m/\ell)^{1/2^m}
       +\sqrt{H(\gamma+H^2/A_0)}.
 \label{eq:six-errors}
\end{aligned}$$ For class $0$, the corresponding bound is $$\label{eq:zero-errors}
 \mathbb E\mathfrak d_X(L^0)
 \le e_{\mathrm{loc}}+\frac{2\sigma^{-2}}{A_0}
      +2A_0(m/\ell)^{1/2^m}.$$

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-box-cs-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-box-cs</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:inner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:inner">7</a>.* The smoothing parameters were fixed in Proposition <a href="#prop:correlation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:correlation">9</a>. Choose $m$ as in <a href="#eq:choose-m" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:choose-m">[eq:choose-m]</a>, define $d_0$ by <a href="#eq:class-target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:class-target">[eq:class-target]</a>, and set $e=d_0/10$. Make the following successive choices, each with strict slack:

1.  Choose a positive integer $H$ with $\sigma^{-2}/H<e$.

2.  Choose a positive rational $\gamma$ with $H\gamma<e^2/4$.

3.  Choose a positive integer $A_0$ so large that $2\sigma^{-2}/A_0<e$ and $H^3/A_0<e^2/4$. These choices give $\sqrt{H(\gamma+H^2/A_0)}<e$.

4.  Choose an integer $\ell\ge2$ so large that $2A_0(m/\ell)^{1/2^m}<e$.

5.  Choose an integer $n>\ell$ so large that $\sigma^{-1}\sqrt{(s+Rm)\ell/(n-1)}<e$.

6.  Now $N=n^m$, $M=mn^{m-1}$ and $C_0=4^M\binom{N+s}{s}$ are fixed. Choose positive rationals $p,\theta<1$ so small that $$\theta^2+\frac{mpN}{\sigma^2}
            <\frac{e^4\sigma^4\gamma^2}{C_0^2}.$$ This makes $e_{\mathrm{atom}}<e$.

All choices are finite and use explicit inequalities. Roots in these inequalities can be removed by raising positive quantities to integer powers, so rational arithmetic and integer search suffice. Neither the alphabet size nor the distribution of the proof index enters them.

If the row-atom assertion failed for these $p,\theta$, the preceding argument would apply. Each pair-class correlation in <a href="#eq:six-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:six-errors">[eq:six-errors]</a> would be less than $6e<d_0$, and the class-$0$ correlation in <a href="#eq:zero-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:zero-errors">[eq:zero-errors]</a> would be less than $3e<d_0$. Summing over the $1+\binom m2$ classes would give $$\mathbb E\mathfrak d_X(L)<(1+\tbinom m2)d_0=\frac1{4T},$$ contradicting <a href="#eq:class-correlation-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:class-correlation-lower">[eq:class-correlation-lower]</a>. This proves the claimed probability at least $p$ of an odd conditional coefficient of magnitude at least $\theta$. The pointwise bound on $h_x$ is <a href="#eq:gradient-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gradient-bounds">[eq:gradient-bounds]</a>, completing Theorem <a href="#thm:inner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:inner">7</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:outer"></a>
### An outer game with affine hints

This Section constructs the outer game and proves the two properties used in Section <a href="#sec:composition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:composition">6</a>: soundness with shared affine hints, and approximate joint uniformity of pulled-back affine functions.

Smooth equation–variable sampling and Fourier decoding have antecedents in Khot and Safra (Khot and Safra 2013, secs. 3.2–3.4 and 5.2) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[KS13](#ref-KS13)]. Khot, Minzer and Safra (Khot, Minzer, and Safra 2025, secs. 3.3–3.4) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[KMS17](#ref-KMS17)] use shared linear information and zero-advice coordinates; Dinur et al. (Dinur et al. 2025, sec. 5.2, Lemma 5.4) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[DKKMS2025](#ref-DKKMS2025)] give a related shared-subspace formulation. We prove both properties for the exact questions and hints defined below.

Let $\mathcal E=(E^{(1)},\ldots,E^{(M_0)})$ be the nonempty uniform equation list supplied by Theorem <a href="#thm:max3lin" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:max3lin">4</a>. An equation question $E$ consists of its ordered three variable names $v(E,1),v(E,2),v(E,3)$ and its right-hand side $\tau_E\in\mathbb F_2$. Thus it asks that $$x_{v(E,1)}+x_{v(E,2)}+x_{v(E,3)}=\tau_E.$$ The list may have repeated equations, and variable names may repeat within an equation. Sampling is uniform over list entries, but equal equation questions have identical encodings: an index distinguishing equal list entries is not part of a player’s question.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### Questions, fresh slots, and canonical functions

For an equation $E$, put $$A(E)=\{a\in\mathbb F_2^3:
        a_1+a_2+a_3=\tau_E,\quad
        a_p=a_{p'}\text{ whenever }v(E,p)=v(E,p')\}.$$ This is a nonempty affine space of dimension at most two. Indeed, after identifying repeated variable names, there are $k\le3$ free bit slots and one nonzero linear equation: at least one multiplicity is odd because the sum of the multiplicities is three. The equation therefore has rank one, and $\dim A(E)=k-1$.

Fix integers $t\ge t_0\ge1$. An *outer sample* $e$ is drawn as follows. First draw $t$ independent uniform list entries, with resulting equation questions $E_1,\ldots,E_t$, and let $$U=(E_1,\ldots,E_t),\qquad A_U=\prod_{j=1}^t A(E_j).$$ Next draw a uniform $t_0$-element subset $H\subseteq[t]$. For each $j\in H$, independently draw $p_j\in[3]$ uniformly. The second question is the ordered, tagged tuple $V=(V_1,\ldots,V_t)$, where $$V_j=\begin{cases}
       (\mathrm{variable},v(E_j,p_j)),&j\in H,\\
       (\mathrm{equation},E_j),&j\notin H.
     \end{cases}$$ In particular, a variable question contains its variable name and the coordinate $j$ in the ordered tuple, but neither its position $p_j$ nor its originating equation. Set $$A_V=\prod_{j=1}^t A_{V,j},\qquad
 A_{V,j}=\begin{cases}\mathbb F_2,&j\in H,\\\mathbb F_2^3,&j\notin H.\end{cases}$$ Every displayed slot in this product is unrestricted. Even when the same variable name occurs more than once, no equalities between these slots are imposed. Likewise, the slots belonging to distinct coordinates of $A_U$ are separate; its only equalities between repeated names are those inside a single factor $A(E_j)$. These fresh slots are essential both to independent repetition and to the distribution of affine forms below.

<a id="eq:outer-projection"></a>
Define the affine map $\pi_e:A_U\to A_V$ coordinatewise by $$\label{eq:outer-projection}
 (\pi_e(a))_j=\begin{cases}(a_j)_{p_j},&j\in H,\\a_j,&j\notin H.\end{cases}$$ The verifier accepts labels $a\in A_U$ and $b\in A_V$ precisely when $\pi_e(a)=b$. The map need not be onto: the unrestricted full triples on the second side need not satisfy their equations. Pullback is the linear map $$\pi_e^*:D(A_V)\longrightarrow D(A_U),\qquad L\longmapsto L\circ\pi_e,$$ and preserves the constant-one function.

We specify exactly what a question and an affine hint reveal. Every actual $U$ or $V$ is the tuple just defined, without extra information identifying its occurrence in $e$. Put its alphabet in lexicographic order as a subset of its displayed bit slots. A function on that alphabet is represented by its complete truth table in this order. In particular, a hint in $D(A_U)$ or $D(A_V)$ is the function itself, with this canonical encoding. It is not accompanied by a formula, its original coefficients on another alphabet, or an encoding of the map through which it was pulled back. Equal functions on the same actual question have equal encodings. The same convention will apply to Boolean queries in the bit proofs. Thus a response function at a given question cannot distinguish different descriptions of the same query.

For an integer $q\ge0$, the *game with $q$ affine hints* additionally draws independent uniform functions $L_1,\ldots,L_q\in D(A_V)$. The first player receives $$(U,\pi_e^*L_1,\ldots,\pi_e^*L_q)$$ and the second receives $(V,L_1,\ldots,L_q)$. The acceptance condition is still $\pi_e(a)=b$. Giving independent shared or private randomness to these players does not change the upper bounds on its value.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Soundness despite the affine hints

Consider first the ordinary equation-versus-variable game: draw a uniform list entry $E$, choose a uniform position $p\in[3]$, give $E$ to the first player and only $v(E,p)$ to the second, and ask for $a\in A(E)$ and a bit $b$. Accept when $a_p=b$. Equivalently the first answer alphabet can be $\mathbb F_2^3$ with invalid answers rejected, so the answer sizes are at most eight and two.

<a id="stmt-lem-base-equation-game"></a>
<div id="lem:base-equation-game" class="lemma">

**Lemma 12**. *If no assignment satisfies more than $3/4$ of the equation list, the ordinary equation-versus-variable game has value at most $11/12$. Consequently its ordinary $r$-fold repetition has value at most $c^r$, for the effective universal constant $c<1$ of Theorem <a href="#thm:parallel-repetition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:parallel-repetition">5</a>, for every $r\ge0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-base-equation-game"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-base-equation-game</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a deterministic second strategy. Its bit for each variable name defines a global assignment. On at least $1/4$ of the sampled equations this assignment violates the equation. Every valid first answer to one of these equations disagrees with this assignment in at least one of the three positions, so a uniform position detects disagreement with probability at least $1/3$. An invalid answer is rejected in every position. The overall failure probability is therefore at least $1/12$. Repeated variable names do not affect the argument: equality at all three positions would still give precisely the violated assignment on that equation. The repeated bound follows from Theorem <a href="#thm:parallel-repetition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:parallel-repetition">5</a>; its hypotheses depend only on this value bound and the two answer sizes. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-hint-soundness"></a>
<div id="lem:hint-soundness" class="lemma">

**Lemma 13** (Hint soundness). *Suppose no assignment satisfies more than $3/4$ of the equation list. For every $q\ge0$ and $t\ge t_0\ge1$, the value of the game with $q$ affine hints is at most <a id="eq:hint-bound"></a>$$\label{eq:hint-bound}
 \bigl(1-2^{-q}(1-c)\bigr)^{t_0}.$$ The bound also applies to strategies using arbitrary independent shared or private randomness. In particular, for fixed $q$ and any prescribed rational $s>0$, an effective choice of $t_0$ makes the bound strictly less than $s$, simultaneously for every $t\ge t_0$, every equation-list size, and every completeness parameter in Theorem <a href="#thm:max3lin" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:max3lin">4</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-hint-soundness"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-hint-soundness</summary>

<a id="eq:hint-coefficients"></a>
*Proof.* Use the unrestricted slots of $A_V$ to generate the hints. Let $z_j$ denote the one-bit coordinate when $j\in H$, and let $z_{jp}$, $p\in[3]$, denote the three coordinates when $j\notin H$. A uniform affine form has the unique expression $$\label{eq:hint-coefficients}
 L_\ell(z)=\kappa_\ell+
       \sum_{j\in H}\beta_{\ell j}z_j+
       \sum_{j\notin H}\sum_{p=1}^3\beta_{\ell jp}z_{jp}.$$ All coefficients and constants in these expressions, for $1\le\ell\le q$, are independent uniform bits. They can be sampled after $H$ and before any equation or position, because the abstract slots in this expression depend only on $H$. Thus their distribution is independent of all sampled equations and positions.

<a id="eq:active-binomial"></a>
Define the active coordinates by $$A=\{j\in H:\beta_{\ell j}=0\text{ for every }1\le\ell\le q\}.$$ For each $j\in H$ its vector of $q$ coefficients is uniform in $\mathbb F_2^q$, independently across $j$. Consequently $$\label{eq:active-binomial}
 |A|\sim\operatorname{Bin}(t_0,2^{-q}).$$ Conditional on $H$, the active set and the coefficient bits are independent of the equation and position samples. The equations are also independent of $H$. When $q=0$, all hidden coordinates are active and <a href="#eq:active-binomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:active-binomial">[eq:active-binomial]</a> means $|A|=t_0$ deterministically.

For the analysis give both players the following additional information: $H$, every coefficient and constant in <a href="#eq:hint-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hint-coefficients">[eq:hint-coefficients]</a>, and all equation samples and all chosen positions outside $A$. Denote this background by $W$. It determines $A$ without inspecting its equation or position samples. Conditional on any possible $W$, the pairs $$(E_j,p_j),\qquad j\in A,$$ are still independent samples of a uniform equation-list entry and an independent uniform position in $[3]$. This follows directly from the product sampling distribution: once $H$ and the coefficients are fixed, $A$ is fixed, and conditioning on samples in its complement does not change the samples in $A$.

We now show that, for fixed $W$, ordinary repeated-game questions in the active coordinates simulate each player’s original information exactly. List $A$ in increasing order, so that its $|A|$ coordinates can be identified with an ordinary repetition.

1.  The first player receives the active equation questions $(E_j)_{j\in A}$. Together with $W$, these reconstruct all of $U$ and hence $A_U$. For every hint the active summands in <a href="#eq:hint-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hint-coefficients">[eq:hint-coefficients]</a> are identically zero. On any $a\in A_U$, therefore, $$(\pi_e^*L_\ell)(a)=\kappa_\ell+
        \sum_{j\in H\setminus A}\beta_{\ell j}(a_j)_{p_j}+
        \sum_{j\notin H}\sum_{p=1}^3\beta_{\ell jp}(a_j)_p.$$ Every position appearing on the right is in the background. Thus the first player can compute the canonical truth table of each pulled-back hint, without receiving any active $p_j$ or active variable question from the second side. Although the domain $A_U$ depends on active equations, these are exactly the first player’s own repeated-game questions.

2.  The second player receives only the active variable names $(v(E_j,p_j))_{j\in A}$. Together with $W$ and the tags specified by $H$, these reconstruct $V$ and $A_V$. Formula <a href="#eq:hint-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hint-coefficients">[eq:hint-coefficients]</a>, whose active coefficients vanish, computes every direct hint on this alphabet. Thus the second player can compute its canonical truth table without receiving an active equation or the chosen position within one.

These reconstructions also explain why the representation of a hint matters. No metadata attached to a zero summand is present in the actual message. Restricting the zero function to an affine domain cannot create an offset or convey a hidden position. All nonzero contributions and their possible affine offsets are determined by the background and the receiving player’s own question.

Apply the original response functions to the reconstructed information, and restrict their answers to the active coordinates. This defines a strategy for the ordinary $|A|$-fold repeated game, with $W$ fixed as a parameter. Acceptance of the full outer sample implies acceptance in every active coordinate, since <a href="#eq:outer-projection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:outer-projection">[eq:outer-projection]</a> imposes exactly the ordinary equation-versus-variable comparison there. By Lemma <a href="#lem:base-equation-game" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:base-equation-game">12</a>, its conditional probability is at most $c^{|A|}$; for $A=\varnothing$ this is the trivial bound one.

This reasoning includes coincidences between variable names or equation questions in different coordinates, and coincidences with the exposed background. Independent samples may coincide without ceasing to have a product distribution. Once $W$ is fixed, the simulated strategies may use all information about such coincidences obtainable from their respective questions, just as arbitrary strategies for ordinary repetition may do. No equality between fresh answer slots, nor any further information about an active question on the opposite side, has been supplied.

Average over $W$ and use <a href="#eq:active-binomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:active-binomial">[eq:active-binomial]</a>. The binomial generating function gives $$\mathbb P[\text{outer acceptance}]
 \le\mathbb Ec^{|A|}
 =\sum_{r=0}^{t_0}\binom{t_0}{r}(2^{-q}c)^r
                       (1-2^{-q})^{t_0-r}
 =\bigl(1-2^{-q}(1-c)\bigr)^{t_0}.$$ One may first fix all independent random seeds of the players, apply the bound, and then average; this covers the stated random strategies.

Finally replace $c$, if necessary, by an effectively chosen rational number $\bar c$ with $c\le\bar c<1$. Successively multiplying the rational number $1-2^{-q}(1-\bar c)<1$ finds an integer $t_0$ whose power is strictly less than $s$. This finite procedure is independent of $t$, the instance, and its completeness error. It supplies the asserted effective choice. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### Joint marginal smoothness

The following estimate concerns the joint distribution of several forms pulled back through the same outer sample. Conditioning is on $U$ only; in particular it is not conditioning on any previously received hint. For finite probability distributions $\mu,\nu$, use $\operatorname{TV}(\mu,\nu)=\frac12\sum_x\lvert\mu(x)-\nu(x)\rvert$.

<a id="stmt-lem-smoothness"></a>
<div id="lem:smoothness" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:smoothness-bound"></a>
**Lemma 14** (Joint smoothness). *Fix any actual first question $U$, integers $d\ge1$ and $t\ge t_0\ge1$, and draw the remaining outer sample conditional on $U$. Draw $L_1,\ldots,L_d$ independently and uniformly from $D(A_V)$. Then $$\label{eq:smoothness-bound}
 \operatorname{TV}\left(\mathcal L\bigl((\pi_e^*L_1,\ldots,\pi_e^*L_d)\mid U\bigr),
                 \operatorname{Unif}(D(A_U)^d)\right)
 \le\frac12\sqrt{(2^{4dt_0}-1)\frac{t_0^2}{t}}.$$ The bound is uniform in $U$ and the equation list, including repeated variable names and zero-dimensional local alphabets. For fixed $d,t_0$ and positive rational $\Delta$, an effective integer $t\ge t_0$ makes the right-hand side strictly less than $\Delta$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-smoothness"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-smoothness</summary>

*Proof.* Write $A_j=A(E_j)$ and $r_j=\dim A_j\le2$. Choose, using $E_j$ alone, an affine origin $o_j\in A_j$ and an injective linear map $T_j:\mathbb F_2^{r_j}\to\mathbb F_2^3$ with image the translation space of $A_j$. Thus every label has a unique expression $$a_j=o_j+T_ju_j,\qquad u_j\in\mathbb F_2^{r_j}.$$ For example, lexicographic search and binary Gaussian elimination make these choices canonical and effective. In these product coordinates a form on $A_U$ consists of one constant bit and a linear part in each coordinate group $\mathbb F_2^{r_j}$. A $d$-tuple of forms consequently consists of $d$ constant bits and groups $$\xi_j\in(\mathbb F_2^{r_j})^d,\qquad 1\le j\le t.$$

Generate the $d$ forms by their independent raw coefficients and constants on the unrestricted slots of $A_V$, as in <a href="#eq:hint-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hint-coefficients">[eq:hint-coefficients]</a>. After pullback, their constant bits are the original independent uniform constants plus offsets determined by the other coefficients, the positions, and the origins $o_j$. Conditional on all those other data, this addition is a translation of $\mathbb F_2^d$. Hence the resulting constants are jointly uniform and independent of every linear part, of $H$, and of the chosen positions. Their common uniform factor contributes no total-variation distance, so it suffices to analyze the joint linear parts $(\xi_1,\ldots,\xi_t)$.

Fix $H$. If $j\notin H$, a single form has independent uniform coefficient vector $w\in\mathbb F_2^3$ on its three raw slots; its pulled-back linear part is $T_j^{\mathsf T}w$. The map $T_j^{\mathsf T}$ has rank $r_j$ and is onto $\mathbb F_2^{r_j}$. Every fiber therefore has the same size, so its output is uniform. All $d$ forms in this group are independent, and their joint law is the uniform law $\mu_j$ on $(\mathbb F_2^{r_j})^d$.

<a id="eq:local-density"></a>
If $j\in H$, condition first on its chosen position $p_j$. The $\ell$th form has a uniform bit coefficient $\beta_{\ell j}$ on that slot, so its linear part is $\beta_{\ell j}v_{j,p_j}$, where $v_{j,p}$ is the linear part of the $p$th coordinate of $o_j+T_ju_j$. Average over the single uniform choice of $p_j$ shared by all $d$ forms, to obtain a distribution $Q_j$ on $(\mathbb F_2^{r_j})^d$. Its density $\rho_j$ with respect to $\mu_j$ satisfies $$\label{eq:local-density}
 \mathbb E_{\mu_j}\rho_j=1,\qquad
 0\le\rho_j\le2^{r_jd}\le2^{2d}.$$ Indeed, every point of this finite space has uniform mass $2^{-r_jd}$, whereas its $Q_j$ mass is at most one. This argument allows a position’s linear part to be zero or to coincide with another position’s linear part. If $r_j=0$, the space is a singleton and $\rho_j=1$.

For fixed $H$, the linear-part groups are independent across $j$: their coefficients are independent, and the positions in distinct hidden coordinates are independently sampled. Fresh slots give this independence even when variable names in different equations coincide. Relative to the product uniform measure $\mu=\prod_{j=1}^t\mu_j$, their joint density is therefore $$g_H(\xi_1,\ldots,\xi_t)=\prod_{j\in H}\rho_j(\xi_j).$$ The densities $\rho_j$ depend on $U,d,j$ but not on the other elements of $H$. Since $H$ is uniform and independent of $U$, the desired mixed density is $g=\mathbb E_H g_H$.

Let $H'$ be an independent uniform $t_0$-element subset of $[t]$. If $H\cap H'=\varnothing$, independence of the coordinate groups and <a href="#eq:local-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-density">[eq:local-density]</a> give $\mathbb E_\mu g_Hg_{H'}=1$. For every $H,H'$, the pointwise bound $g_Hg_{H'}\le2^{4dt_0}$ gives the sufficient bound $\mathbb E_\mu g_Hg_{H'}\le2^{4dt_0}$. Moreover, for fixed $H$, $$\mathbb P[H\cap H'\ne\varnothing]
 \le\sum_{j\in H}\mathbb P[j\in H']
 =\frac{t_0^2}{t}.$$ Since $\mathbb E_\mu g=1$, it follows that $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E_\mu(g-1)^2
 &=\mathbb E_{H,H'}\mathbb E_\mu g_Hg_{H'}-1\\
 &\le(2^{4dt_0}-1)\mathbb P[H\cap H'\ne\varnothing]\\
 &\le(2^{4dt_0}-1)\frac{t_0^2}{t}.
\end{aligned}$$ Cauchy–Schwarz now yields $\operatorname{TV}(g\mu,\mu)=\frac12\mathbb E_\mu\lvert g-1\rvert
\le\frac12\sqrt{\mathbb E_\mu(g-1)^2}$, proving <a href="#eq:smoothness-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:smoothness-bound">[eq:smoothness-bound]</a>, including the independent uniform constants.

All estimates used only $r_j\le2$, so the bound is uniform as asserted. For effectiveness, it is enough to choose an integer $$t\ge t_0,\qquad
 t>\frac{(2^{4dt_0}-1)t_0^2}{4\Delta^2}.$$ The right-hand side is an explicitly computable rational number for fixed $d,t_0,\Delta$. No parameter depending on $U$ occurs in this choice. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rem-joint-smoothness-use"></a>
<div id="rem:joint-smoothness-use" class="remark">

*Remark 15*. Because <a href="#eq:smoothness-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:smoothness-bound">[eq:smoothness-bound]</a> is uniform in $U$, it may be averaged over any distribution of $U$ and any extra randomness independent of the outer sample. In particular, if a bounded statistic is chosen as a function of $U$ and some of the pulled-back forms, one may apply Lemma <a href="#lem:smoothness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:smoothness">14</a> to the joint collection of all forms used by that statistic. For a statistic bounded in absolute value by $B$, the change in expectation is at most $2B$ times the displayed total-variation bound. This does not assert uniformity of one form conditional on previously revealed forms.

</div>

Finally, these outer games are explicit finite objects. Their complete sampling space has $$M_0^t\binom{t}{t_0}3^{t_0}$$ equally likely draws, counting repeated list entries with their multiplicities. There are at most $2^{2t}$ labels at a first question and $2^{3t-2t_0}$ labels at a second question. Their affine spaces, functions, canonical truth tables, and projections are all enumerable by binary linear algebra and finite evaluation. For fixed $t,t_0,q,d$ these local enumerations have constant size independent of the input. Comparing actual question encodings and identifying equal ones takes polynomial bit complexity. Thus the fresh-slot and canonical-function conventions introduce no oracle or uncharged lookup table.

<a id="sec:composition"></a>
### Composition and soundness of the bit comparisons

We now compose the inner test with the outer game. The main issue is that the first player’s Fourier truncation depends on the shared background. The joint form of Lemma <a href="#lem:smoothness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:smoothness">14</a> is designed to retain that dependence.

<a id="stmt-prop-bit-gap"></a>
<div id="prop:bit-gap" class="proposition">

**Proposition 16** (A gap for bit comparisons). *For every fixed integer $J\ge20$, effective choices of constants give a four-test comparison system with the following properties. It is constructed from the uniform equation list in Theorem <a href="#thm:max3lin" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:max3lin">4</a>, for a fixed completeness error $\zeta>0$ chosen by the construction. Every test compares two bits in folded proof tables, and its contribution to cost is its failure probability divided by a positive rational budget $b_j$. In the YES case there are proof tables of cost at most $4$; in the NO case every collection of proof tables has cost greater than $J$. Alphabet sizes are bounded by effective constants depending only on $J$, and all local numerical parameters depend only on $J$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-bit-gap"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-bit-gap</summary>

*Proof.* Apply Theorem <a href="#thm:inner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:inner">7</a> with $J$, taking its atom constants $p,\theta$ to be positive rationals by decreasing them if necessary. Set $$M=mn^{m-1},\qquad q=M-1,\qquad M_*=\frac{\sqrt N}{\sigma}.$$ The counts $t_0\le t$ of the outer game will be chosen below. Give each actual first question $U$ a folded bit proof $f_U$ on $A_U$, and each actual second question $V$ a folded bit proof $f_V$ on $A_V$. The same question always uses the same proof; an occurrence of a hidden variable is never separately indexed by its unrevealed equation or position.

<a id="eq:fourth-test"></a>
The first three tests are those of Theorem <a href="#thm:inner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:inner">7</a>, applied to $f_V$ under the marginal distribution of $V$. For the fourth test sample a full outer edge $e$, an independent uniform face array $B$ over $D(A_V)$, and the Gaussian query table $P=P_{c+\sigma g,\eta\lambda}$ on the common code $\mathcal C$. Compare $$\label{eq:fourth-test}
 f_U\bigl(P(\pi_e^*B)\bigr)
 \quad\hbox{and}\quad
 f_V\bigl(P(B)\bigr).$$ Here $\pi_e^*$ acts on every entry of $B$. The Gaussian table on $\mathcal C$ is literally the same on both sides, even if distinct codewords have identical pullbacks at one alphabet. Let $b_4>0$ be a small rational budget, to be fixed shortly, and define $$\operatorname{Cost}(f)=\sum_{j=1}^4\frac{\mathbb P[\text{test $j$ fails}]}{b_j}.$$

<a id="eq:HV"></a>
<a id="eq:HU"></a>
Suppose, toward a contradiction in the NO case, that $\operatorname{Cost}(f)\le J$. Each of the first three failure bounds required by Theorem <a href="#thm:inner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:inner">7</a> then holds, including the random index $V$. Draw an outer edge $e$, a uniform $i\in[m]$ and $x\in X$, and independent $c$. The $q$ rows other than $B^i_{x_{-i}}$ will serve as the independent uniform background forms allowed in Lemma <a href="#lem:hint-soundness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hint-soundness">13</a>. Put them in a fixed order depending only on the public $i,x$. The two players receive these rows on their own alphabets, and use $c,i,x$ as additional public randomness, independent of the game. From their respective information they form the one-row functions $$\begin{aligned}
 H_V(b)&=h_{x,V}\bigl(B[B^i_{x_{-i}}\leftarrow b],c\bigr),
      &&b\in D(A_V),\label{eq:HV}\\
 H_U(a')&=h_{x,U}\bigl((\pi_e^*B)
                 [B^i_{x_{-i}}\leftarrow a'],c\bigr),
      &&a'\in D(A_U).\label{eq:HU}
\end{aligned}$$ The notation in <a href="#eq:HU" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:HU">[eq:HU]</a> simply means filling the missing entry of the received array with a free argument. The first player needs no knowledge of $e$ or $V$ to define $H_U$. Both functions are bounded in absolute value by $M_*$.

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
###### The two row functions are close.

<a id="eq:row-comparison"></a>
For fixed $e,B,c$, apply <a href="#eq:gaussian-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gaussian-derivative">[eq:gaussian-derivative]</a> to the difference between the two bits in <a href="#eq:fourth-test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fourth-test">[eq:fourth-test]</a>, using the same $g,\lambda$. Bessel’s inequality for the orthonormal functions $g_x$ gives $$\frac1N\sum_x
 \lvert h_{x,V}(B,c)-h_{x,U}(\pi_e^*B,c)\rvert^2
 \le\frac1{\sigma^2}\mathbb E_{g,\lambda}
 \lvert f_V(P(B))-f_U(P(\pi_e^*B))\rvert^2.$$ Averaging $e,B,c$ bounds the right-hand side by $4Jb_4/\sigma^2$. Integrating the missing row in <a href="#eq:HV" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:HV">[eq:HV]</a> restores a fully independent uniform array $B$, regardless of $i,x$. Thus $$\label{eq:row-comparison}
 \mathbb E\lVert H_V-H_U\circ\pi_e^*\rVert_{L^2(D(A_V))}^2
 \le\frac{4Jb_4}{\sigma^2}.$$ Choose a positive rational $b_4$ so small that <a id="eq:budget-choice"></a>$$\label{eq:budget-choice}
 \frac{64Jb_4}{\sigma^2\theta^2}<\frac p4.$$ Markov’s inequality then makes the norm in <a href="#eq:row-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:row-comparison">[eq:row-comparison]</a> less than $\theta/4$, except on an event of probability less than $p/4$.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
###### Diffuse first-side frequencies remain small after pullback.

<a id="eq:remainder-bounds"></a>
Choose a positive rational $u<1$. At each first-player information state, let $r_U$ be $H_U$ with all Fourier terms of magnitude at least $u$ removed. Parseval and the bound $\lvert H_U\rvert\le M_*$ give $$\label{eq:remainder-bounds}
 \#\{\alpha:\lvert\widehat H_U(\alpha)\rvert\ge u\}
 \le\frac{M_*^2}{u^2},\qquad
 \sum_\alpha\lvert\widehat r_U(\alpha)\rvert^4\le u^2M_*^2,
 \qquad
 \lVert r_U\rVert_\infty\le M_r:=M_*+\frac{M_*^2}{u}.$$ For the last bound, each removed coefficient satisfies $\lvert a\rvert\le\lvert a\rvert^2/u$.

<a id="eq:restricted-fourth"></a>
We claim that if the total-variation bound of Lemma <a href="#lem:smoothness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:smoothness">14</a> for $d=q+3$ is at most $\Delta$, then $$\label{eq:restricted-fourth}
 \mathbb E\sum_{\beta\in D(A_V)^*}
       \lvert\widehat{r_U\circ\pi_e^*}(\beta)\rvert^4
 \le u^2M_*^2+2\Delta M_r^4.$$ To prove this, fix $U$ and the independent public randomness $c,i,x$. For fixed background and $e$, identity <a href="#eq:fourth-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fourth-moment">[eq:fourth-moment]</a> expresses the Fourier sum on the left as the average of $$r_U(\pi_e^*a)r_U(\pi_e^*b)r_U(\pi_e^*c')
 r_U\bigl(\pi_e^*(a+b+c')\bigr),$$ where $a,b,c'$ are independent uniform forms on $A_V$. Pullback is linear on the spaces of affine forms, so its fourth argument is determined by the first three. The entire expression, including the choice of $r_U$, depends only on $U,c,i,x$ and the $q+3$ pulled-back forms consisting of the background and these three additional arguments. It is bounded by $M_r^4$ in absolute value. Replace their joint distribution by independent uniform forms on $A_U$. Lemma <a href="#lem:smoothness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:smoothness">14</a> changes the expectation by at most $2\Delta M_r^4$. Under the replacement, conditioning on the background fixes $r_U$, and <a href="#eq:fourth-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fourth-moment">[eq:fourth-moment]</a> and <a href="#eq:remainder-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:remainder-bounds">[eq:remainder-bounds]</a> bound its expectation by $u^2M_*^2$. Averaging proves <a href="#eq:restricted-fourth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:restricted-fourth">[eq:restricted-fourth]</a>. This argument uses joint total variation; it does not conditionally replace the missing row while keeping a nonuniform background fixed.

First choose $u>0$ rational sufficiently small, and then require a sufficiently small positive rational $\Delta$, so that <a id="eq:remainder-choice"></a>$$\label{eq:remainder-choice}
 \frac{256\bigl(u^2M_*^2+2\Delta M_r^4\bigr)}{\theta^4}<\frac p4.$$ By <a href="#eq:restricted-fourth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:restricted-fourth">[eq:restricted-fourth]</a> and Markov’s inequality, no Fourier coefficient of $r_U\circ\pi_e^*$ has magnitude at least $\theta/4$, except on an event of probability less than $p/4$.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
###### Local lists give a strategy for the outer game.

Theorem <a href="#thm:inner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:inner">7</a> supplies an odd coefficient $\beta$ of $H_V$ with magnitude at least $\theta$ with probability at least $p$. Combining that event with the two preceding estimates leaves probability at least $p/2$. On this event, the coefficient of $H_U\circ\pi_e^*$ at $\beta$ has magnitude greater than $3\theta/4$, since every coefficient is bounded by the $L^2$ norm of a function. The coefficient of its remainder has magnitude less than $\theta/4$. Consequently the pullback of the removed part has a nonzero coefficient at $\beta$. At least one removed frequency $\alpha$ therefore satisfies $$(\pi_e^*)^*\alpha=\beta.$$ This conclusion permits multiple frequencies to have the same image: a nonzero sum has at least one nonzero summand. Since $\pi_e^*(1)=1$ and $\beta$ is odd, such an $\alpha$ is odd as well.

<a id="eq:decoder-success"></a>
The first player lists the odd frequencies of $H_U$ of magnitude at least $u$, and the second lists the odd frequencies of $H_V$ of magnitude at least $\theta$. These lists are functions of their own information. By Lemma <a href="#lem:odd-labels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:odd-labels">3</a>, they are lists of labels, and on the event just described they contain a pair agreeing under $\pi_e$. Their sizes are at most $M_*^2/u^2$ and $M_*^2/\theta^2$. Choose uniformly from nonempty lists and choose any fixed label when a list is empty. With independent private randomness, the resulting strategy succeeds with probability at least $$\label{eq:decoder-success}
 s_*:=\frac{p/2}{(1+M_*^2/u^2)(1+M_*^2/\theta^2)}>0.$$ The strategy is used only to bound the value of a finite game. Its Fourier computations need not be efficient, and its independent continuous public randomness does not raise the game value above the maximum deterministic value.

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
###### Parameter order and contradiction.

All quantities so far depend only on the inner constants and $J$. Choose $t_0$ effectively so that the upper bound in Lemma <a href="#lem:hint-soundness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hint-soundness">13</a> is strictly below $s_*$ for the fixed $q$. Then choose $t\ge t_0$ so large that Lemma <a href="#lem:smoothness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:smoothness">14</a> gives the required $\Delta$ for $d=q+3$. Equation <a href="#eq:decoder-success" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:decoder-success">[eq:decoder-success]</a> contradicts the hinted-game upper bound, proving that no NO-case proof has cost at most $J$.

Finally choose a positive rational $\zeta$ with $t\zeta\le b_4$ and invoke Theorem <a href="#thm:max3lin" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:max3lin">4</a>. Its NO bound is always $3/4$, independently of $\zeta$, so this last choice does not affect the repetition constants. In the YES case let a global assignment satisfy at least $1-\zeta$ of the equations. Give each $V$ its values at all requested slots. Give $U$ these values whenever all its equations are satisfied, and choose any label in $A_U$ otherwise. The first labels lie in their required affine alphabets, and the labels agree under $\pi_e$ except on an event of probability at most $t\zeta$. Use evaluation proofs at these labels. Lemma <a href="#lem:honest" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:honest">6</a> bounds each of the first three normalized costs by one; agreement of labels makes the fourth comparison exact, so its normalized cost is also at most one. This proves the YES bound.

The order of choices is, explicitly, $$J\ \longrightarrow\ \text{inner constants}
 \longrightarrow\ b_4,u,\Delta,s_*
 \longrightarrow\ t_0\ \longrightarrow\ t\ \longrightarrow\zeta.$$ All strict inequalities can be imposed with rational slack and integer searches using the displayed bounds. In these searches $M_*^2=N/\sigma^2$ is rational; the possibly irrational quantity $M_r$ may be bounded above by the rational number $N/\sigma+N/(\sigma^2u)$ before choosing $\Delta$. The complete construction therefore has effective constants independent of the input length. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:finite"></a>
### A deterministic finite reduction to simple graphs

Fix an integer $K\ge2$, put $J=10K$, and make the effective parameter choices in Proposition <a href="#prop:bit-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:bit-gap">16</a>. Write $\operatorname{Cost}_\infty$ for its cost, and put $$W=\sum_{j=1}^4 b_j^{-1}.$$ This is a positive rational constant depending only on $K$. The Gaussian randomness will now be replaced by one finite rational distribution. We retain the full joint distribution of the queried sign tables.

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### Rational Gaussian sign tables

Let $c,g\in\mathbb R^X$ and $\lambda\in\mathbb R^{\mathcal C}$ have independent standard Gaussian coordinates. Define the two tables on $\mathcal C$ $$\begin{aligned}
 T_0(z)&=\operatorname{sgn}\left(N^{-1/2}\sum_{x\in X}c_x(-1)^{z_x}\right),\\
 T_1(z)&=\operatorname{sgn}\left(N^{-1/2}\sum_{x\in X}(c_x+\sigma g_x)(-1)^{z_x}
                         +\eta\lambda_z\right).
\end{aligned}$$ Their pair is a random element of a finite set of size $2^{2|\mathcal C|}$. All four tests are deterministic functions of this pair and their finite random choices. The first uses both tables. The second uses the same $T_1$ for $B$ and $B'$. The third forces one entry of $T_1$ to the two specified values. The fourth uses the same $T_1$ on the two outer alphabets. Thus a single approximation of $(T_0,T_1)$ suffices.

<a id="stmt-lem-finite-game"></a>
<div id="lem:finite-game" class="lemma">

**Lemma 17** (Effective finite comparisons). *There is a deterministic construction, with all preliminary work depending only on $K$, of a finite rational replacement for the four tests such that <a id="eq:uniform-finite-error"></a>$$\label{eq:uniform-finite-error}
 \lvert\operatorname{Cost}_{\mathrm{fin}}(f)-\operatorname{Cost}_\infty(f)\rvert<\frac12
 \qquad\text{for every collection of folded proof tables }f.$$ Consequently its minimum cost is less than $9/2$ in the YES case, and every proof has cost greater than $10K-1/2$ in the NO case. The rational distribution replacing $(T_0,T_1)$ has a positive integer common denominator depending only on $K$, computed by the construction.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-finite-game"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-finite-game</summary>

*Proof.* We give a deterministic rational approximation with any prescribed rational accuracy $0<\varepsilon<1/4$. Set $$d=2N+|\mathcal C|,\qquad S=2|\mathcal C|,\qquad A=2N+1.$$ The $S$ unforced threshold inputs are linear forms in a vector $Z\in\mathbb R^d$ of independent standard Gaussians. Each input for $T_0$ has variance $1$, and each input for $T_1$ has variance $1+\sigma^2+\eta^2$. Its coefficient vector has $\ell^1$ norm at most $$(1+\sigma)\sqrt N+\eta\le A.$$ These bounds do not require nonsingularity of the joint distribution of the threshold inputs.

Choose an integer $T_G\ge1$ such that $$d/T_G^2<\varepsilon/4.$$ The probability $\alpha$ that $Z\notin[-T_G,T_G]^d$ is at most $d/T_G^2$, by the second-moment bound in each coordinate and a union bound. Partition $[-T_G,T_G]$ into $L$ equal intervals of length $h=2T_G/L$, with rational midpoints. Choose the integer $L$ large enough that <a id="eq:gaussian-mesh-choice"></a>$$\label{eq:gaussian-mesh-choice}
 SAh<\varepsilon/4.$$ Endpoint conventions for the intervals are immaterial to their Gaussian masses. Rounding an interior vector to its cell midpoint changes any threshold input by at most $Ah/2$. If the corresponding signs change, the original threshold input has absolute value at most $Ah/2$. A centered Gaussian of variance at least one has density at most one, so the probability of this event for any one threshold is at most $Ah$. The union bound over all $S$ thresholds is therefore at most $SAh$. In particular, all unrounded ties have probability zero.

<a id="eq:tail-and-grid-error"></a>
For completeness, this estimate can be combined with truncation without losing control at the boundary. Couple $Z$ with a vector $Y$ as follows: keep $Y=Z$ when $Z$ is in the cube, and otherwise draw $Y$ independently from the Gaussian conditioned on the cube. Then $Y$ has exactly that conditional law. On the event that $Z$ is outside, charge the full probability $\alpha$; on its complement use the preceding sign-change estimate. Hence the law of the original joint sign table and the law obtained by rounding the conditioned Gaussian differ in total variation by at most $$\label{eq:tail-and-grid-error}
 d/T_G^2+SAh<\varepsilon/2.$$

We next compute rational approximations to the conditioned Gaussian cell masses, including their exact normalization. This step uses no real-number oracle. Put $U=T_G^2/2$, and choose an integer $r\ge0$ such that $$e_r:=\frac{U^{2r+1}}{(2r+1)!}
 \quad\text{satisfies}\quad 2dT_G e_r<\varepsilon/4.$$ This is a terminating search with rational comparisons. Indeed $e_{r+1}/e_r=U^2/((2r+2)(2r+3))$, so after an effectively found index the ratio is at most $1/2$. Define the rational polynomial $$Q_r(v)=\sum_{j=0}^{2r}\frac{(-1)^jv^{2j}}{2^j j!}.$$ Taylor’s remainder for $e^{-u}$, with $u=v^2/2\ge0$, gives <a id="eq:positive-density-polynomial"></a>$$\label{eq:positive-density-polynomial}
 0\le Q_r(v)-e^{-v^2/2}\le e_r
 \qquad (|v|\le T_G).$$ In particular $Q_r$ is strictly positive on the interval. For each grid interval $I=[a,b]$, compute the rational number <a id="eq:rational-cell-mass"></a>$$\label{eq:rational-cell-mass}
 w_I=\frac{\displaystyle\int_a^b Q_r(v)\,\mathrm dv}
            {\displaystyle\int_{-T_G}^{T_G} Q_r(v)\,\mathrm dv}.$$ Both integrals are evaluated by the rational antiderivative $$\sum_{j=0}^{2r}\frac{(-1)^jv^{2j+1}}{2^j j!(2j+1)}.$$ Thus the $w_I$ are positive rationals and their sum is exactly one.

Here is a quantitative error bound for these cell masses. Write $g(v)=e^{-v^2/2}$ on $[-T_G,T_G]$, let $Z_g=\int_{-T_G}^{T_G}g$, and let $E_r=\int_{-T_G}^{T_G}(Q_r-g)$. Since $T_G\ge1$ and $e^{-u}\ge1-u$ for $u\ge0$, $$Z_g\ge\int_{-1}^1(1-v^2/2)\,\mathrm dv>1,
 \qquad 0\le E_r\le2T_G e_r.$$ The probability density $Q_r/(Z_g+E_r)$ is a mixture of $g/Z_g$ and the normalized nonnegative residual $Q_r-g$, with residual mixture weight $E_r/(Z_g+E_r)$ (if $E_r=0$ the densities coincide). Their total variation distance is therefore at most $2T_G e_r$. The Gaussian conditioned on the cube is a product of these one-dimensional conditioned laws. Taking products increases the total variation error by at most the sum of the coordinate errors, as follows by replacing the coordinates one at a time. Rounding to the grid cannot increase total variation. Consequently the grid distribution assigning mass $\prod_{i=1}^d w_{I_i}$ to a cell has total variation distance at most $2dT_G e_r<\varepsilon/4$ from the rounded conditional Gaussian law. Together with <a href="#eq:tail-and-grid-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-and-grid-error">[eq:tail-and-grid-error]</a>, this proves error less than $\varepsilon$ for the complete joint sign table.

Every sign at a rational midpoint is also computed exactly. A $T_0$ input has the sign of a rational number. Multiplying a $T_1$ input by $\sqrt N>0$ expresses it as $$a+b\sqrt N\qquad(a,b\in\mathbb Q).$$ If $a,b$ have the same sign, or one is zero, its sign is immediate. If their signs are opposite, compare the rationals $a^2$ and $Nb^2$; the term of larger magnitude determines the sign, and equality is an exact zero. Declare the sign of zero to be $+1$. This also handles square $N$. Thus grid ties and all algebraic comparisons use finite rational arithmetic.
