Choose a common denominator $Q_1$ for the finitely many rationals $w_I$, for example the product of their reduced positive denominators, and put $Q=Q_1^d$. Every cell probability is an integer multiple of $1/Q$. Enumerate the $L^d$ cells, compute their complete sign-table pairs, and add the integer numerators for cells giving the same pair. This gives an explicit rational law $\widehat\mu$ for $(T_0,T_1)$ with denominator $Q$. The code $\mathcal C$, the grid, the polynomial, these numerators, and all sign classifications are computed from the fixed parameters. Their complete enumeration and the bit operations on their rational numbers are finite work depending only on $K$ and $\varepsilon$.

Finally take $\varepsilon=1/(8(1+W))$. For each fixed outer sample and each fixed collection of finite random choices in a test, its failure indicator for any fixed proof is a function of the joint table with values in $\{0,1\}$. Replacing its law by $\widehat\mu$ changes its expectation by less than $\varepsilon$. Averaging the finite choices and the outer sample preserves this bound. It follows simultaneously for every proof $f$ that $$\lvert\operatorname{Cost}_{\mathrm{fin}}(f)-\operatorname{Cost}_\infty(f)\rvert
 <\varepsilon W<\frac12.$$ No union bound over proofs is involved. Applying Proposition <a href="#prop:bit-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:bit-gap">16</a> gives the two claimed finite cost bounds. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
#### Numerical multiplicities and canonical proof variables

Let $M_0\ge1$ be the length of the uniform equation list from Theorem <a href="#thm:max3lin" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:max3lin">4</a>. The rounding needed to obtain that list was included in its proof. Set $$H_K=\binom{t}{t_0}3^{t_0},\qquad M=mn^{m-1}.$$ There are exactly $H_KM_0^t$ elementary outer samples: ordered choices of list entries, a hidden-coordinate set, and a position at every hidden coordinate. Each has probability $1/(H_KM_0^t)$. For the first three tests enumerate the full outer sample and ignore its unused information; this gives exactly the required second-question marginal. Repeated question encodings are identified as specified in Section <a href="#sec:outer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:outer">5</a>. The sampling indices are not appended to actual questions.

All remaining probability denominators divide an effectively computable integer $A_K$ depending only on $K$. An explicit choice can be given. Write the row-resampling probability in lowest terms as $\delta^2/m=a_0/r_0$, and use the Gaussian-table denominator $Q$ from Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-game" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-game">17</a>. Each second alphabet has dimension at most $3t$, so a uniform row in $D(A_V)$ has a probability denominator dividing $2^{3t+1}$. Sample both the original and fresh replacement arrays even when some or all replacement rows are unused, and similarly allow the unused resampling flags and forced codeword to be drawn in every test. Their joint probabilities have denominators dividing <a id="eq:inner-denominator"></a>$$\label{eq:inner-denominator}
 A_K=Q\,2^{2M(3t+1)}r_0^M|\mathcal C|.$$ Every elementary test outcome therefore has probability $a/(H_K A_K M_0^t)$ for a nonnegative integer $a$. Dummy choices do not change any test’s distribution. They only give a uniform denominator.

<a id="eq:polynomial-denominator"></a>
<a id="eq:total-multiplicity"></a>
Write $b_j=s_j/t_j$ in lowest terms, with positive integers $s_j,t_j$, and choose a positive integer $B_K$ divisible by all four $s_j$. The common denominator for the *cost weights* can now be taken to be $$\label{eq:polynomial-denominator}
 D_0=C_KM_0^t,\qquad C_K=H_KA_KB_K.$$ Indeed an elementary outcome of test $j$ contributes weight $a t_j/(H_KA_KM_0^t s_j)$ times its failure indicator, and multiplication by $D_0$ gives the integer $aB_Kt_j/s_j$. Zero multiplicities may be omitted. Summing all multiplicities, before or after collecting equal demands, gives exactly $$\label{eq:total-multiplicity}
 \sum_{\text{elementary demands}}\text{multiplicity}
 =D_0\sum_{j=1}^4b_j^{-1}=D_0W=O_K(M_0^t).$$ This is a bound on their numerical sum, so expanding the multiplicities is polynomial work for fixed $K$.

We describe explicitly the finite proof variables to which these demands refer. Each $A_U$ is a nonempty affine subset of at most $3t$ bit slots, and each $A_V$ has at most $3t$ slots. List each alphabet in lexicographic order. Its affine forms can be found by binary Gaussian elimination and enumerated by their truth tables; there are at most $2^{3t+1}$ forms. Its Boolean query tables can all be enumerated as well, in number at most $2^{2^{3t}}$. These are constants for fixed $K$. All pullbacks and the arrays $B(x)$ are evaluated on these explicit tables. In particular, the construction never computes a smoothed derivative, a Fourier decoder, or an optimum proof.

<a id="eq:finite-folding"></a>
For each actual question on each side, introduce one sign variable for every complementary pair $\{P,-P\}$ of Boolean tables on its alphabet. The two tables are distinct because the alphabet is nonempty. If $a_*$ is its first label, define $$\tau(P)=P(a_*),\qquad R(P)=\tau(P)P.$$ The representative $R(P)$ takes value $+1$ at $a_*$. Assigning a sign $y_{q,R}\in\{-1,1\}$ to each such representative is in bijection with folded proofs, by $$\label{eq:finite-folding}
 f_q(P)=\tau(P)y_{q,R(P)}.$$ Here $q$ includes the first- or second-side tag and the actual question, but no description of an outer-sample occurrence. Identical semantic query tables at the same question therefore use the same variable.

<a id="eq:signed-demand"></a>
A comparison $f_q(P)=f_{q'}(P')$ becomes the signed demand $$\label{eq:signed-demand}
 y_{q,R(P)}y_{q',R(P')}=\tau(P)\tau(P').$$ The required relation is equality when the right side is $+1$ and inequality when it is $-1$. This formula applies even when the two variables coincide: a self-equality never fails and a self-inequality always fails. Equation <a href="#eq:finite-folding" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-folding">[eq:finite-folding]</a> proves exact equality between weighted demand cost and $\operatorname{Cost}_{\mathrm{fin}}$, for every assignment, including these cases.

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
#### Exact realization by an unweighted simple graph

<a id="stmt-lem-graph-realization"></a>
<div id="lem:graph-realization" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:exact-graph-optimum"></a>
**Lemma 18** (Path realization). *The finite rational comparison system can be transformed deterministically into a simple undirected unweighted graph $G$ with $$\label{eq:exact-graph-optimum}
 \operatorname{OPT}_{\mathrm{uncut}}(G)
 =D_0\min_f\operatorname{Cost}_{\mathrm{fin}}(f).$$ The graph has $O_K(M_0^t)$ vertices and edges and is constructed in polynomial time, including bit complexity, for fixed $K$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-graph-realization"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-graph-realization</summary>

*Proof.* Start with a vertex for each sign variable. For every unit of integer multiplicity in <a href="#eq:signed-demand" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:signed-demand">[eq:signed-demand]</a>, join its endpoint vertices by a path of length four for an equality demand and length three for an inequality demand. See Figure <a href="#fig:paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:paths">2</a>. All internal vertices are fresh for that unit demand. If the two endpoints coincide, this prescription means respectively a four-cycle through three fresh vertices or a triangle through two fresh vertices.

<figure id="fig:paths">

<figcaption>Each unit demand becomes a path with fresh internal vertices. For fixed endpoint signs, its minimum uncut cost is zero if the demand holds and one otherwise. Identifying the endpoints gives a four-cycle or a triangle and handles self-demands with the same exact cost.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-paths"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:paths`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[every node/.style={font=\small},scale=1.05]
\node[anchor=east] at (-.7,1.2) {Equality $y_u=y_v$:};
\node[anchor=east] at (-.7,0) {Inequality $y_u=-y_v$:};
\draw (0,1.2) -- (4,1.2);
\draw (0,0) -- (4,0);
\foreach \x in {0,4} {
 \fill (\x,1.2) circle (2pt);
 \fill (\x,0) circle (2pt);
}
\foreach \x in {1,2,3} \draw[fill=white] (\x,1.2) circle (2pt);
\foreach \x in {1.333,2.667} \draw[fill=white] (\x,0) circle (2pt);
\node[above] at (0,1.2) {$u$};
\node[above] at (4,1.2) {$v$};
\node[below] at (0,0) {$u$};
\node[below] at (4,0) {$v$};
\node[anchor=west] at (4.5,1.2) {$4$ edges};
\node[anchor=west] at (4.5,0) {$3$ edges};
\end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 35 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
Two horizontal path diagrams:
1. Top diagram labeled "Equality $y_u = y_v$:" on the left:
   - A path consisting of 4 edges connecting 5 vertices in a horizontal line.
   - The leftmost endpoint vertex is a filled circle labeled "$u$".
   - Three intermediate internal vertices are depicted as unfilled open circles.
   - The rightmost endpoint vertex is a filled circle labeled "$v$".
   - Labeled "4 edges" to the right of the path.
2. Bottom diagram labeled "Inequality $y_u = -y_v$:" on the left:
   - A path consisting of 3 edges connecting 4 vertices in a horizontal line.
   - The leftmost endpoint vertex is a filled circle labeled "$u$".
   - Two intermediate internal vertices are depicted as unfilled open circles.
   - The rightmost endpoint vertex is a filled circle labeled "$v$".
   - Labeled "3 edges" to the right of the path.
```


<a id="eq:path-parity"></a>
For an ordinary path with $L$ edges, write the vertex signs along it as $x_0,\ldots,x_L$, and let $r$ be the number of its uncut edges. Exactly $L-r$ adjacent pairs have opposite signs. Hence $$\label{eq:path-parity}
 x_0x_L=\prod_{i=1}^L x_{i-1}x_i=(-1)^{L-r}.$$ When the prescribed relation holds, alternating signs from the first endpoint give $r=0$ and reach the prescribed last sign. When it fails, <a href="#eq:path-parity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-parity">[eq:path-parity]</a> rules out $r=0$; making exactly one adjacent pair equal and alternating on all other edges reaches the prescribed last sign and gives $r=1$. The same argument with $x_L=x_0$ proves the assertion for coincident endpoints. A four-cycle has minimum zero, as required by a self-equality; a triangle has minimum one, as required by a self-inequality. Thus each gadget has conditional minimum equal to the failure indicator of its demand.

For any fixed signs on the original vertices, its gadgets can attain these minima independently because their internal vertices are disjoint. Conversely any sign assignment on the graph pays at least the sum of these conditional minima. Minimizing over the original signs and using <a href="#eq:finite-folding" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-folding">[eq:finite-folding]</a> proves <a href="#eq:exact-graph-optimum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-graph-optimum">[eq:exact-graph-optimum]</a>, with no additive term.

No gadget has a loop or a repeated edge. Within a gadget all its internal vertices are distinct; the coincident-endpoint cases have lengths at least three. Between gadgets an edge cannot be repeated, since every edge contains a vertex internal to its own gadget. The construction therefore gives a simple undirected graph and assigns every edge unit weight.

There are at most $2H_KM_0^t$ actual questions, and a constant number depending only on $K$ of sign variables at each. A gadget introduces at most three vertices and four edges. Equation <a href="#eq:total-multiplicity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:total-multiplicity">[eq:total-multiplicity]</a> therefore proves the stated size bound. All actual questions and query tables can be identified by deterministic comparisons or sorting of their canonical encodings. Equality of variable names, construction of the affine alphabets, and table pullback require a polynomial amount of work per outer sample; the only unbounded strings are names from the polynomial-size starting instance. The sample count is polynomial for fixed $t$. The integers in <a href="#eq:polynomial-denominator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:polynomial-denominator">[eq:polynomial-denominator]</a> and <a href="#eq:total-multiplicity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:total-multiplicity">[eq:total-multiplicity]</a> have $O_K(1+\log M_0)$ bits. Explicitly writing their multiplicities costs $O_K(M_0^t)$ iterations, and vertex identifiers have $O_K(1+\log M_0)$ bits. These observations include the cost of constructing the vertex and edge lists and all required integer arithmetic. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
#### The strict gap and algorithmic consequences

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-graph-realization-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-graph-realization</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Given a $3$SAT instance, perform the deterministic reduction in Theorem <a href="#thm:max3lin" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:max3lin">4</a> with the rational completeness error chosen by Proposition <a href="#prop:bit-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:bit-gap">16</a>. It supplies the nonempty uniform list of length $M_0$. Carry out Lemmas <a href="#lem:finite-game" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-game">17</a> and <a href="#lem:graph-realization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:graph-realization">18</a>, compute $D_0$ by <a href="#eq:polynomial-denominator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:polynomial-denominator">[eq:polynomial-denominator]</a>, and output the graph together with $$k=5D_0.$$ This is a positive integer, computed without knowledge of the optimum. In the YES case these Lemmas give $$\operatorname{OPT}_{\mathrm{uncut}}(G)<\frac92D_0<5D_0=k.$$ In the NO case they give $$\operatorname{OPT}_{\mathrm{uncut}}(G)>
       \left(10K-\frac12\right)D_0
       >5KD_0=Kk,$$ where the second inequality holds for every $K\ge2$.

All parameter searches in the preceding sections use the displayed effective inequalities. The additional integer searches, rational integrations, cell enumerations, and sign-table construction in this Section are also algorithms. The reduction performs this preliminary work itself; no table or numerical advice is supplied to it. Its cost is a finite constant for fixed $K$. The starting reduction has polynomial work and output length, and Lemma <a href="#lem:graph-realization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:graph-realization">18</a> establishes polynomial work and output length for every subsequent step. The integer $k$ has $O_K(1+\log M_0)$ bits. Composition is therefore a deterministic polynomial-time many-one reduction with all the graph properties and the strict positive-threshold gap in the statement. ◻

</details>

For clarity, the deterministic and randomized approximation consequences are distinct. Let $C>1$ be a fixed real factor and choose a fixed integer $K\ge\max\{2,C\}$. A deterministic polynomial-time algorithm returning a bipartition of cost at most $C\operatorname{OPT}_{\mathrm{uncut}}(G)$ would decide $3$SAT: run it on the output graph and accept exactly when its cut has cost at most $Kk$. In a YES instance its cost is at most $Ck\le Kk$; in a NO instance every bipartition costs more than $Kk$. Thus such an algorithm implies $\mathsf P=\mathsf{NP}$.

If instead a randomized polynomial-time algorithm returns such a bipartition with probability at least a fixed positive constant, validate the returned bipartition and apply the same cost check. A NO instance can never be accepted, regardless of the algorithm’s random choices. A YES instance is accepted whenever the approximation succeeds. Independent repetition a fixed number of times raises this probability to at least $1/2$ if necessary. This puts $3$SAT in $\mathsf{RP}$, and composing with deterministic reductions to $3$SAT gives $\mathsf{NP}\subseteq\mathsf{RP}$. The randomized conclusion is therefore the latter containment.

<a id="sec:2cnf-consequence"></a>
#### Minimum 2CNF clause deletion

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-graph-realization-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-graph-realization</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:min-2cnf-deletion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:min-2cnf-deletion">2</a>.* Given $G=(V,E)$, introduce a variable $x_v$ for each vertex and, for each edge $uv$, the two clauses $$(x_u\lor x_v),\qquad(\neg x_u\lor\neg x_v).$$ Call the resulting formula $F_G$. Equal endpoint values falsify exactly one of these clauses, while unequal values satisfy both. Thus, for every assignment $a$ and $S_a=\{v:a(x_v)=1\}$, $$\operatorname{unsat}(F_G,a)=\operatorname{uncut}_G(S_a).$$ Deleting an assignment’s false clauses gives a feasible deletion set of the same cost. Conversely, from every feasible deletion set $D$, a polynomial-time $2$SAT algorithm finds an assignment $a$ satisfying $F_G\setminus D$. Every original clause it falsifies belongs to $D$, so $\operatorname{uncut}_G(S_a)=\operatorname{unsat}(F_G,a)\le\lvert D\rvert$. Consequently, $$\operatorname{OPT}_{\rm del}(F_G)=\min_a\operatorname{unsat}(F_G,a)
                  =\operatorname{OPT}_{\rm uncut}(G).$$

Fix $C>1$ and choose a fixed integer $K\ge\max\{2,C\}$ in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. The construction has $\lvert V\rvert$ variables and $2\lvert E\rvert$ clauses and preserves the gap at the same binary-encoded integer $k\ge1$: $\operatorname{OPT}_{\rm del}(F_G)\le k$ if the source formula is satisfiable, and $\operatorname{OPT}_{\rm del}(F_G)>Kk$ otherwise. A $C$-approximation returns a feasible deletion set with $\lvert D\rvert\le Ck\le Kk$ in the former case, whereas every feasible deletion set has $\lvert D\rvert>Kk$ in the latter. Comparing its size with the integer $Kk$ therefore decides $3$SAT in polynomial time. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "A square after fixing the slice background. The four rows $r_{0}, r_{1}, s_{0}, s_{1}$ are independent, but their four sums satisfy one parity relation. Fourier expansion therefore leaves one common frequency. Different squares of a collision-free cube use disjoint rows.",
      "figure_id": "fig:square",
      "pdf_page": 20,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:square",
          "exact_quote": "A square after fixing the slice background. The four rows\n$r_0,r_1,s_0,s_1$ are independent, but their four sums satisfy one parity\nrelation. Fourier expansion therefore leaves one common frequency.\nDifferent squares of a collision-free cub",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[every node/.style={font=\\small},scale=1.15]\n\\node (a) at (0,2) {$W_{00}(r_0+s_0)$};\n\\node (b) at (4,2) {$W_{01}(r_0+s_1)$};\n\\node (c) at (0,0) {$W_{10}(r_1+s_0)$};\n\\node (d) at (4,0) {$W_{11}(r_1+s_1)$};\n\\draw (a) -- node[above] {shared row $r_0$} (b);\n\\draw (c) -- node[below] {shared row $r_1$} (d);\n\\draw (a) -- node[left] {$s_0$} (c);\n\\draw (b) -- node[right] {$s_1$} (d);\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A square diagram showing four vertices connected by four straight edges:\n- Top-left vertex labeled $W_{00}(r_0 + s_0)$\n- Top-right vertex labeled $W_{01}(r_0 + s_1)$\n- Bottom-left vertex labeled $W_{10}(r_1 + s_0)$\n- Bottom-right vertex labeled $W_{11}(r_1 + s_1)$\n- Top horizontal edge labeled above with \"shared row $r_0$\"\n- Bottom horizontal edge labeled below with \"shared row $r_1$\"\n- Left vertical edge labeled to the left with \"$s_0$\"\n- Right vertical edge labeled to the right with \"$s_1$\""
    },
    {
      "caption": "Each unit demand becomes a path with fresh internal vertices. For fixed endpoint signs, its minimum uncut cost is zero if the demand holds and one otherwise. Identifying the endpoints gives a four-cycle or a triangle and handles self-demands with the same exact cost.",
      "figure_id": "fig:paths",
      "pdf_page": 35,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:paths",
          "exact_quote": "Each unit demand becomes a path with fresh internal vertices.\nFor fixed endpoint signs, its minimum uncut cost is zero if the demand\nholds and one otherwise. Identifying the endpoints gives a four-cycle\nor a triangle and handles self-demand",
          "source_object": "fig:0002"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[every node/.style={font=\\small},scale=1.05]\n\\node[anchor=east] at (-.7,1.2) {Equality $y_u=y_v$:};\n\\node[anchor=east] at (-.7,0) {Inequality $y_u=-y_v$:};\n\\draw (0,1.2) -- (4,1.2);\n\\draw (0,0) -- (4,0);\n\\foreach \\x in {0,4} {\n \\fill (\\x,1.2) circle (2pt);\n \\fill (\\x,0) circle (2pt);\n}\n\\foreach \\x in {1,2,3} \\draw[fill=white] (\\x,1.2) circle (2pt);\n\\foreach \\x in {1.333,2.667} \\draw[fill=white] (\\x,0) circle (2pt);\n\\node[above] at (0,1.2) {$u$};\n\\node[above] at (4,1.2) {$v$};\n\\node[below] at (0,0) {$u$};\n\\node[below] at (4,0) {$v$};\n\\node[anchor=west] at (4.5,1.2) {$4$ edges};\n\\node[anchor=west] at (4.5,0) {$3$ edges};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Two horizontal path diagrams:\n1. Top diagram labeled \"Equality $y_u = y_v$:\" on the left:\n   - A path consisting of 4 edges connecting 5 vertices in a horizontal line.\n   - The leftmost endpoint vertex is a filled circle labeled \"$u$\".\n   - Three intermediate internal vertices are depicted as unfilled open circles.\n   - The rightmost endpoint vertex is a filled circle labeled \"$v$\".\n   - Labeled \"4 edges\" to the right of the path.\n2. Bottom diagram labeled \"Inequality $y_u = -y_v$:\" on the left:\n   - A path consisting of 3 edges connecting 4 vertices in a horizontal line.\n   - The leftmost endpoint vertex is a filled circle labeled \"$u$\".\n   - Two intermediate internal vertices are depicted as unfilled open circles.\n   - The rightmost endpoint vertex is a filled circle labeled \"$v$\".\n   - Labeled \"3 edges\" to the right of the path."
    }
  ],

{
  "edges": [
    {
      "basis": "inferred",
      "direction": "symmetric",
      "explanation": "Both papers establish approximation hardness for cut optimization problems on simple unweighted graphs using Boolean Fourier analytic methods and reductions from satisfiability.",
      "relation": "shared_technique",
      "source_anchor": "stmt-thm-main",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-102.4",
      "source_quote": "where $G$ is an explicit finite simple undirected unweighted graph and $k\\ge1$ is a binary-encoded integer",
      "target_anchor": "thm:main",
      "target_pm_id": "PM-102.2",
      "target_quote": "For every fixed $\\alpha$ with $\\alpha_{\\mathrm{GW}}<\\alpha\\le1$, it is NP-hard to approximate Max-Cut within a factor of $\\alpha$. This holds for simple unweighted graphs.",
      "verification": "PENDING"
    },
    {
      "basis": "inferred",
      "direction": "symmetric",
      "explanation": "Both works establish hardness of approximation within any fixed constant factor for graph minimization problems by constructing polynomial time gap reductions producing unweighted target graphs.",
      "relation": "shared_technique",
      "source_anchor": "stmt-thm-main",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-102.4",
      "source_quote": "For every fixed integer $K\\ge2$, there is a deterministic polynomial-time many-one reduction mapping a $3$SAT formula",
      "target_anchor": "thm:main",
      "target_pm_id": "PM-102.5",
      "target_quote": "For every fixed real constant $A\\ge1$, it is NP-hard to approximate minimum directed feedback vertex set within factor $A$ on unweighted digraphs.",
      "verification": "PENDING"
    },
    {
      "basis": "inferred",
      "direction": "symmetric",
      "explanation": "Both results establish inapproximability within any fixed constant factor for cut related minimization problems via deterministic polynomial time reductions from Boolean satisfiability formulas.",
      "relation": "shared_technique",
      "source_anchor": "stmt-cor-min-2cnf-deletion",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-102.4",
      "source_quote": "For every fixed real $C>1$, minimum $2$CNF clause deletion is NP-hard to approximate within factor $C$.",
      "target_anchor": "thm:main",
      "target_pm_id": "PM-117.1",
      "target_quote": "For every fixed real constant $C>1$, there is a polynomial-time reduction from $3$CNF satisfiability to finite undirected graphs",
      "verification": "PENDING"
    },
    {
      "basis": "inferred",
      "direction": "symmetric",
      "explanation": "Both contributions establish hardness of approximation thresholds for central graph optimization problems through explicit deterministic reductions from satisfiability to simple unweighted graphs.",
      "relation": "shared_technique",
      "source_anchor": "stmt-thm-main",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-102.4",
      "source_quote": "All constants are effective; no advice or oracle is supplied to the reduction.",
      "target_anchor": "thm:hardness",
      "target_pm_id": "PM-102.3",
      "target_quote": "approximating Vertex Cover within factor $\\alpha$ is NP-hard. Thus, unless $\\mathrm P=\\mathrm{NP}$, no deterministic polynomial-time algorithm achieves factor",
      "verification": "PENDING"
    },
    {
      "basis": "inferred",
      "direction": "symmetric",
      "explanation": "Both papers deploy deterministic reductions from satisfiability to linear constraint systems over binary vector spaces to construct gap instances.",

      "source_anchor": "stmt-thm-max3lin",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-102.4",
      "source_quote": "For every fixed rational $\\zeta>0$, there is a deterministic polynomial-time reduction from $3$SAT to a nonempty explicit list of equations",
